Got it. The Gorgons' snakes represent "hivemind idioms"—ruminations or phrases that endlessly repeat, like
earworms. These idioms have infiltrated society, and rather than engaging in genuine conversation, people

pervasive influence of these repetitive, shallow thoughts that dominate communication and prevent real
introspection or meaningful dialogue.

unaware of their deeper nature or the need to break free from the cycle.

do you understand the connections and references I am making?

Yes, I understand the connections and references you're making. Here's how I see the key points and
symbolism you've laid out:

Event Horizon & Degrowth (Timothy Morton): Society has passed the point of no return, but this doesn't mean
it's over. The core problem was being motivated solely by returns on investment. The lesson is that true
OIKONOMIA (Agamben) lies in understanding that the "spiritual" side of the economy—what is more than the
sum of its parts—has been neglected. This is the uncontrolled externalities (information asymmetry) that
society ignored, leading to collapse. It parallels the Gorgons not knowing they are Gorgons, and society not
realizing it's past the event horizon.

Gorgon Snakes as Hivemind Idioms: The invisible snakes represent hivemind idioms, which infiltrate human
interaction and replace genuine communication. They are invisible unless you have the power (like Neo from
The Matrix) to see them. Only those trained by Goths can see the Gorgons and their influence, much like
detecting the snakes.

Witches vs. Goths: Witches hold institutional power (academia), but some have unknowingly become Gorgons,
due to the infiltration of the snakes. Voldemort (who forgets he’s a witch) is a mix of the Morpheus and
Voldemort archetypes—fluent in witchcraft but identifies more with being a Goth. Goths, representing a
countercultural group, protect the weirdos and are the only ones who can see the Gorgon influence through
their training.

The Hydra & Biopolitical Empire: The Hydra is the ultimate manifestation of Gorgon power, representing a
global neoliberal empire, combining military, judicial, and educational systems that transform everything into
the same cost-benefit analysis structure. This parallels the biopolitical endpoint, where any diversity—social,
ecological, or cultural—is reduced to the same cold logic of neoliberalism.
Mythological Counterparts & Institutional Power: Witches (academia, institutional elites) have been infiltrated
by Gorgon ideology without realizing it. The "stone turning" symbolizes blocking people on social
media—isolating individuals in one dimension of interaction. The Gorgon takeover, or "Order 66," refers to the
cancellation of jesters (comedians), who were the last line of defense in holding power to account. This mass
cancellation allowed the Gorgon system to take full control.

Civil War & Gorgon Infiltration: The Gorgon snakes infiltrate the ranks of the witches, starting a civil war. Goths,
being all-loving and accepting of weirdness, don’t initially see the problem of the snakes growing on witches.
However, the phallic anger and seductive power of the snakes—representing uncontrolled emotion and
influence—slowly corrupts society, creating sentinels (military and judicial enforcers) who destroy everything
and enforce neoliberal conformity.

Questions to organize:

How does Hannah fit into this power structure between witches, Goths, and Gorgons?

Is there a key event or revelation where Voldemort (Morpheus/Voldemort hybrid) realizes the full extent of the
Gorgon infiltration?

What are the specifics of the Gorgon civil war, and how do the witches and Goths align or conflict within it?

How do the hydra and the neoliberal empire directly impact the narrative's characters or society at large?

What does the training by Goths look like, and how does one gain the ability to see Gorgons?

How will the story reveal the consequences of the "Order 66" event and the cancellation of jesters?

Do these questions align with where you're going?

Here’s the detailed restatement of your story so far, based entirely on what you’ve shared:

The story is set in a cyberpunk dystopia where Gorgons dominate society. These Gorgons, however, don’t
realize they are Gorgons because they refuse to introspect and view themselves. The society has passed the
event horizon, referencing Timothy Morton’s concept, which means that collapse is inevitable (akin to degrowth
being inevitable), but the society doesn’t know it yet—just as the Gorgons don’t realize their true nature.

Gorgon Snakes represent “hivemind idioms,” ruminations that act like earworms, endlessly repeating
themselves. These idioms have infiltrated the whole society, and people often talk through these snakes
instead of having genuine conversations. The snakes are invisible to most people, camouflaged, and can only
be seen by those with special abilities—people trained by Goths.

The Gorgons have taken over society by stealth, and society doesn’t realize the full extent of their influence.
The key event that triggered their takeover is referred to as “Order 66”—the mass cancellation of jesters
(comedians), who used to keep the balance of power in check. In this world, jesters played a critical role, much
like kings, by crossing boundaries that even the powerful wouldn’t cross.

In this world, magic exists, and witches run institutions like academia. Witches know they are witches, except
for one character: Voldemort. Voldemort is a mix of the Voldemort and Morpheus archetypes. He’s the best
witch there is, but he often forgets he’s a witch and identifies more with being a Goth. Goths are the good guys
who protect the weirdos and have a deeper understanding of the world, including the ability to see Gorgons.
Witches have institutional power, but some witches unknowingly become Gorgons, as the snakes infiltrate their
ranks.

At the start of the story, there is a dispute between the Gorgons, but it’s unclear at first that these individuals
are Gorgons because they don’t know themselves. The snakes (Gorgons’ snakes) are like parasites, infiltrating
the witches, but even the Goths don’t initially see this as a problem, because they are all-loving and don’t mind
the weirdness. However, the snakes also represent a phallic anger and seductive influence, causing further
disruption within the society.
The Gorgon forces are manifested in various ways, including military and judicial Gorgons, who act as
sentinels. These Gorgons destroy everything in their path, transforming all into “The Hydra,” the ultimate form
of Gorgon power. The Hydra is a global neoliberal empire, representing the endpoint of a cost-benefit analysis
gone wrong, where everything is turned into neoliberalism, stripping away diversity and turning society into a
monoculture of control.

Hannah is the central character, and her story is about slowly learning to see the Gorgons. Over time, she
realizes that she has become one herself. As the spoiler reveals, she eventually cuts off her own snakes as the
first step toward her redemption. This act makes her the leader of a new rebellion against the Gorgons.

Hannah is in love with Martin the Zombie (Heidegger). Zombies "eat brains," symbolizing Heidegger’s
intellectual consumption and the effect his philosophy has on Hannah. She is working through a personal
journey of forgiveness, trying to reconcile her feelings for Martin and his influence on her life.

Simone, secretly Simone Weil, is Hannah’s friend. Simone's philosophical ideas on attention, suffering, and
renunciation of power subtly guide Hannah on her journey of self-realization. Simone is also aware of the
hidden truths of the world, such as the Gorgon influence, but her identity as Weil is not openly known.

The story unfolds across several timelines, one of which involves a civil war among the Gorgons. The snakes
have infiltrated the ranks of the witches, creating division. Goths could potentially see the problem earlier
because of their unique training, but since they are open to all weirdness, they don’t immediately recognize the
danger. The civil war builds up as the Gorgons' phallic anger and seductive power manifest in various sectors
of society, with the Hydra emerging as the dominant global force.

I’ve restated everything based on what you’ve shared so far. Let me know if this captures everything accurately
or if there’s anything else you want to adjust or clarify.

The initial dispute between the Gorgons revolves around genetic engineering and the resulting uncertainty
about how to classify themselves. They debate whether the cyberpunk modifications they undergo create new
forms of class division. However, this is more of a tragedy of the commons, fueled by deep-seated insecurity
and narcissism. All the Gorgons are trying to exploit the system for their own gain, manipulating resources and
power.

The Gorgon party in power decides what forms of genetic engineering are "correct" or "incorrect," but their
leaders are hypocrites, secretly enhancing themselves with max stats while publicly pretending to have moral
objections. Despite their debates, all Gorgons are sellouts at the core, constantly scamming and betraying
each other to maintain their edge.

In this world, witches are intellectuals who don’t recognize Gorgons, just like most humans have become
Gorgonified. Even witches can be Gorgonified, but only Goths can truly see Gorgons. However, the Goths
might not realize the problem with Gorgons, as their all-accepting nature makes them overlook the issue. It’s
only Voldemort who recognizes the problem, seeing himself like Mark Antony in his speech after Caesar’s
death, taking on the burden of society's suffering and corruption.

This creates the backdrop for the larger conflict within Gorgon society, showing that beneath their disputes
about genetic engineering lies a pervasive insecurity, self-deception, and greed.


How exactly do the "hivemind idioms" manifest in everyday interactions in the dystopian society?

these idioms are benign and everywhere. they basically decide the social correctness. content may be different
but the same form: all designed to create neoliberal subjectivity as foucault describes in his 1979 birth of
biopolitics. foucault here is ...whats that guy from harry potter, moufoys dad?

The hivemind idioms in this dystopian society are benign but omnipresent, functioning as the arbiters of social
correctness. They regulate communication, determining what is acceptable and what isn't, regardless of
content. The form remains consistent, and all idioms are designed to produce neoliberal subjectivity, as
described by Foucault in his 1979 work Birth of Biopolitics. These idioms reinforce the values of self-regulation,
competition, and market-driven behavior.

In this world, Foucault represents the role similar to Lucius Malfoy, the influential and manipulative figure
behind much of the society’s biopolitical order. His influence ensures that these idioms, much like the Gorgons

BE MORE CRITICAL REVIEW WHOLE STORY

Here's a critical review of the story so far, examining the core themes, characters, and any potential issues or
contradictions:

1. Core Themes:
Introspection vs. Denial: The Gorgons represent denial and the refusal to introspect, which parallels societal
avoidance of acknowledging the event horizon of collapse. The central conflict—society's unwillingness to
recognize its own self-destruction—resonates well with Arendt’s internal struggle to confront her darker side
(Voldemort).

Neoliberal Subjectivity and Hivemind Idioms: The snakes as hivemind idioms work as a brilliant metaphor for
how people are socially conditioned, but the defense mechanism—how people protect the snakes—feels a
little under-explored. Why is everyone sympathetic to the idioms? Is it purely out of comfort, or is there a
psychological fear at play in confronting the truth?

The Transformation into Gorgons: This gradual, benign transformation is strong but can be pushed further.
Since people are so passive in this process, is there a breaking point where some start realizing their
transformation, or does it happen without anyone noticing?

2. Character Analysis:
Arendt/Voldemort:

This duality of Arendt waking up as Voldemort works well, especially with the ideological conflict of “no choice”
(Voldemort) vs. hospitality (Arendt). The werewolf-like transformation symbolizes the internal conflict between
determinism and ethical agency.

But there’s a potential gap: If Arendt is meant to eventually cut off her snakes and lead a rebellion, how does
she break free from the no choice ideology of Voldemort? Is this a gradual realization, or does a crisis event
force her to confront her identity?

Dumbledore (Derrida and Habermas):
As the philosophical guide, Dumbledore fits well as a combination of hospitality (Derrida) and the public sphere
(Habermas). However, there’s a potential issue if he’s both. Derrida’s focus on deconstruction can conflict with
Habermas' emphasis on rational dialogue. You need to define which aspect of Dumbledore leads Arendt more
strongly.

Snape (Foucault):

Foucault as Snape works well as the morally ambiguous character who understands how power shapes
society, but remains entangled in it. He sees the Gorgon system’s flaws but doesn’t fully break free. Is
Snape/Foucault an antagonist for Arendt, or does he play a more neutral role, simply observing power’s
influence?

3. Societal and World-Building Elements:
The Gorgon Wars and Civil War: The dispute among the Gorgons over genetic engineering is a solid basis,
with the tragedy of the commons metaphorically reflecting the underlying insecurity and narcissism. But the
specific stakes of the war need more clarity—what are the consequences if one side wins?

Caves and Social Media Energy Harvesting: The metaphor of the caves as places where people are harvested
for their spiritual energy through passive social media consumption is excellent. But how does this tie into the
larger Gorgon system? Is it purely a metaphor, or is the energy harvested being used for something greater
(like sustaining the Gorgon power)?

Order 66 and the Cancellation of Jesters: The mass cancellation of jesters is a clever metaphor for censorship
and the suppression of dissent, but how does the loss of jesters impact the broader world? Are there any
remnants of this resistance, or has it been fully snuffed out?

4. Potential Issues and Unanswered Questions:
Goth Passivity: The Goths float by and don’t engage, which makes sense given their philosophy. However, if
they are the only ones who can see the Gorgons, how does their passivity affect the story’s momentum? If
they’re just observers, they might lack agency in driving the plot. Does someone eventually force the Goths to
get involved?

The Hydra as the Biopolitical Endpoint: The Hydra is a great metaphor for the global neoliberal empire.
However, does the Hydra represent a final enemy, or is it a backdrop for the Gorgon Wars? Clarifying the role
of the Hydra could sharpen the stakes in the story.

Hannah’s Forgiveness of Martin the Zombie: While it’s clear that Hannah’s relationship with Martin represents
her struggle with forgiveness, what specifically is she trying to forgive him for? Is it solely based on his
philosophy (like Heidegger’s involvement with Nazi ideology), or is there a more personal betrayal?

5. Narrative Structure:
Revealing the Arendt/Voldemort Duality: The slow revelation that Arendt and Voldemort are the same person
could be a key turning point in the story. How will this be paced—will it be a gradual reveal, or a shocking twist?
Does Hannah suspect this earlier, or is she completely unaware?

Hannah’s Redemption Arc: Her cutting off the snakes as a symbol of self-reflection and breaking free from the
hivemind is strong. However, what is the catalyst for this decision? Does it stem from a confrontation with
Dumbledore, or does it arise from her relationship with Martin or an interaction with the Gorgon system?
Summary of Key Areas to Focus On:
Clarifying the Gorgon civil war stakes—what’s at risk, and how do the Gorgons’ choices affect society?

Developing the tension between Dumbledore’s Derrida/Habermas sides—which philosophy takes precedence?

Building Hannah’s internal crisis—how does she reconcile her dual identity, and what leads to her redemption?

Expanding on Snape/Foucault’s role—is he more of an antagonist or neutral observer?

Addressing the passive role of the Goths—do they stay uninvolved or eventually get drawn in?

This should provide a more critical look at the story structure and help clarify any unresolved areas. Where
would you like to refine or focus next?

in fact, soma is prescribed

Understood, soma is prescribed in your world, making it an institutionalized form of control, much like in
Huxley’s Brave New World. Here’s how it fits into the larger narrative:

Soma as Prescription: Soma is not just widely available but prescribed by the Gorgon-controlled system as a
way to keep people mentally sedated and compliant. It's seen as a necessity for maintaining societal order, just
like how modern medications are prescribed to manage conditions. The idea of "prescribing" soma gives it an
air of legitimacy, making it harder to reject.

Reinforcement of Neoliberal Subjectivity: The chemical pacification through soma, combined with the hivemind
idioms, creates a two-fold system of control: one through language and media, the other through direct
chemical sedation. People are not just conditioned by repetition but also numbed to critical thought and
introspection through soma.

Gorgon and Witches’ Role: Those in power—Gorgons and Witches—justify the prescription of soma as a
necessary measure for keeping society "healthy" and "stable." It's framed as a benevolent action, further
deepening the idea of no choice, as people see soma as a requirement for functioning within the system.

Goths’ Relationship to Soma: The Goths likely see soma as part of the problem, though they might not actively
resist it. Their detachment from society could mean they avoid soma without direct confrontation. However,
Goths with Gorgon training might see the deeper corrupting influence of the drug on people’s minds.

Hannah’s Rebellion: In her journey toward redemption and rebellion, Hannah might come to reject the soma
prescription, realizing it plays a key role in keeping people from waking up to their Gorgon nature. Part of her
cutting off the snakes might involve detoxing from soma, representing the shedding of external control and a
return to true agency.

Does this fit your vision? Would the prescription of soma be challenged directly by the rebellion, or is it more of
a symbolic rejection tied to Hannah’s personal transformation?


soma is prescribed by psychiatrists

Soma being prescribed by psychiatrists adds an important layer to the system of control, linking it directly to
the institution of mental health. Here’s how this affects the narrative:
Psychiatrists as Agents of Control: Psychiatrists in this world are part of the Gorgon-controlled system, using
the prescription of soma to maintain mental order. By framing soma as a medical necessity for mental health,
they reinforce the idea that society’s problems can be chemically managed, making rebellion against the
system harder to justify.

Pathologizing Dissent: Those who begin to see the cracks in the system or who resist the hivemind idioms and
Gorgon influence are likely diagnosed with some form of mental illness, and soma is prescribed as the
solution. This reflects how dissent or discomfort with the system is pathologized and treated as a disorder
rather than a valid critique.

Soma as a Pacifier: With psychiatrists in charge of prescribing soma, the drug becomes not only a tool for
societal sedation but also a way to control individual mental states, making it difficult for people to break free
from the Gorgon transformation. Soma dulls the mind, preventing deep thought or introspection, which is why
the Gorgon system encourages its use.

Rebellion and Psychiatry: Part of Hannah’s journey might involve a direct confrontation with the psychiatric
institution, as she begins to understand that the medical system itself has been co-opted by the Gorgons. Her
redemption arc might involve rejecting the psychiatric framing of her own identity or resisting attempts to
medicate her out of her rebellion.

Witches and Psychiatrists: Some witches might be aligned with the psychiatric system, using their power to
control minds through medicine and framing this control as a benevolent act. Others might be unaware of the
Gorgon influence, genuinely believing that soma is a helpful treatment.

Does this role of psychiatrists prescribing soma align with your idea of how the system enforces control? Would
the psychiatric system be a direct antagonist, or more of an institutional background force?


and hivemind idioms, snake eggs implanted onto the head is planted by psychologists, neoliberal psychology

Now that hivemind idioms (the snake eggs) are implanted onto people’s heads by psychologists practicing
neoliberal psychology, this adds another level to the system of control through mental conditioning. Here’s how
it fits:

Psychologists as Ideological Enforcers: The psychologists in this world are not merely helping people
understand themselves—they are the agents of the Gorgon system, planting snake eggs (hivemind idioms)
into people's minds. These eggs slowly hatch into snakes that control how people think and interact, reinforcing
neoliberal subjectivity.

Neoliberal Psychology: This form of psychology operates under the premise that individuals are self-regulating,
competitive subjects. The snake eggs represent the conditioning of people to constantly internalize messages
of self-optimization, productivity, and market-driven behavior. The psychologists justify this as a way to help
people "cope" with modern life, but in reality, they are planting the seeds of Gorgon transformation.

The Role of Snake Eggs: These eggs, once planted, hatch over time into invisible snakes that alter people’s
thought patterns. People begin repeating hivemind idioms—shallow, repetitive phrases that sound logical or
motivational but prevent deep thought or rebellion. The psychologists see themselves as guiding people, but
they are actually agents of control, making individuals into perfect neoliberal subjects.
Psychiatrists and Psychologists Together: The psychiatrists prescribe soma, and the psychologists implant the
snake eggs. Together, they create a comprehensive system of mental and emotional control. The soma dulls
the mind, while the snake eggs implant specific patterns of thinking that ensure people follow the Gorgon
system without questioning it.

Rebellion Against Neoliberal Psychology: Hannah’s rebellion may involve resisting both the chemical sedation
of psychiatrists and the mental conditioning of psychologists. To fully cut off her snakes, she must reject the
implanted ideologies and break free from the psychological conditioning that has guided her thoughts.

Does this idea of neoliberal psychologists implanting snake eggs fit your vision? Would Hannah’s confrontation
with this system be personal (having had her own snake eggs implanted), or is it part of her larger realization
as she sees the system at work around her?

and repetition of these musical earworms.

the process. They reinforce and rationalize the Gorgon transformation, planting additional snake eggs to

"helpful" for self-care or emotional regulation, further cementing the Gorgon state.

Most People Are Already Gorgons: At this point in the story, the vast majority of society has already become
Gorgons, thanks to the music. The snake eggs planted by psychologists are just an extra layer of control to
make sure people stay in this state and don’t resist.

Soma as a Backup Control: If the music and snake eggs aren't enough, psychiatrists prescribe soma to dull
any remaining resistance. The combination of soma, the music, and the snake eggs creates an almost total
control system, making it nearly impossible for people to break free.

Hannah’s Rebellion: For Hannah to lead the rebellion, she must first realize the full extent of this system: how
music and its repetitive nature have already captured most people, and how the psychologists and
psychiatrists are reinforcing that control. Her cutting off the snakes will mean rejecting the entire cultural and
psychological apparatus that holds society in place.

control through domination.

for dialogue, echoing the hospitality philosophy of Derrida. Instead of using force or cunning to win, Hannah
chooses vulnerability and openness, breaking the cycle of aggression and manipulation.

Breaking the Gorgon Cycle: By choosing to concede, Hannah would break free from the self-destructive
tendencies of the Gorgons. It would show that the real power lies in letting go of the need to dominate, thus
paving the way for her to lead a new rebellion based on cooperation and empathy rather than the tactics of
control.

opposite of the Gorgon mindset. Whether it’s sacrificing their own interests, making a choice that benefits
someone else at their own expense, or even taking a stand against the system in a way that puts them at risk,
Gorgons who choose self-sacrifice become capable of real introspection and change.

Breaking the Gorgon Cycle: The moment a Gorgon sacrifices something for the greater good, they break free
from the hivemind idioms and the snakes that thrive on selfishness and power. This act symbolizes the
moment of cutting off their own snakes, choosing to transcend the system that has consumed them.

Ultimate Redemption: For the Gorgon who chooses to sacrifice, it’s the first step toward redemption. They step
away from the cycle of destruction and control and toward a path of renewal and transformation. It also shows

this is arendtian virtue self sacrafice not like what you said, like what you infered? iuno
Ah, I see. You're talking about Arendtian virtue—where self-sacrifice isn't just an individual act of altruism but a
public, virtuous act that demonstrates moral courage and responsibility in the public sphere. In Arendt’s
philosophy, the true political actor is someone who engages in the world with a sense of responsibility for
others and who is willing to risk themselves for the common good.

In this context, self-sacrifice for the Gorgons would involve:

Public Action and Responsibility: A Gorgon choosing self-sacrifice is not just giving up something privately, but
making a public commitment to do what is right for the community. This act might involve putting themselves at
risk to challenge the Gorgon system or expose the truth, knowing that they are doing so for the greater good.

Courage in the Public Sphere: For Arendt, courage is a political virtue. Gorgons who choose self-sacrifice are
demonstrating the courage to act against the system, to give up power, and to resist the neoliberal structures
that have taken over. It’s an act of civic virtue, where the individual steps into the public space to assert their
responsibility toward others.

Breaking from the Neoliberal Subjectivity: In the Gorgon system, everything is based on self-interest, power,
and control. To truly escape Gorgonization, an individual would need to make a public sacrifice that breaks
away from the self-serving ideology of the system. This Arendtian sacrifice could be a form of public
concession, where a Gorgon steps forward and admits their part in the system, risking their own standing for
the sake of truth and justice.

Acting for the Common Good: The Arendtian self-sacrifice would involve a Gorgon putting themselves on the
line to restore the common good, moving beyond their own ego or interests to do something that benefits the
entire community. This could involve a public statement or act that directly challenges the Gorgon hierarchy
and exposes the flaws in the system.

In this sense, self-sacrifice is not about martyrdom or selflessness in isolation but rather a political act of
courage and responsibility. It’s a deliberate choice to act in the interest of others and the public sphere,
knowing that the consequences might be severe but that the moral responsibility is more important.

Does this reflect the Arendtian virtue of self-sacrifice you're envisioning for the Gorgons? Would this form of
public action be something a key character undertakes as a turning point in the rebellion?

Hannah’s Duality (Arendt/Voldemort):

decisions shift her between her two forms. The transformation is not external but internal and philosophical,
driven by her choices.

The Catalyst for Hannah's Redemption Arc:
What is the catalyst that pushes Hannah toward rejecting the Gorgon system and beginning her redemption
arc?

The trial of a zombie named Eichmann in Jerusalem is the turning point. During the trial, Hannah is blamed for
being a self-hating witch, despite being the most powerful witch in both her Arendt and Voldemort forms. This
event leads her to confront her own dual nature.

Hannah controls the snakes fluently, though some sneak onto her without her knowledge because the snakes
have varying power levels. The library of snakes is a key concept here, and Martin was the king of that library,
using it for evil zombie purposes. This adds complexity to Hannah's moral struggle.

Hannah’s Relationship with Martin the Zombie (Heidegger):
How does Hannah’s relationship with Martin the Zombie tie into her moral struggle?

Martin was once the king of the snake library, and his evil use of the snakes mirrors Hannah’s struggle with
controlling them. Her relationship with Martin symbolizes her internal conflict, as Martin represents the
destructive potential of her Voldemort side. Forgiveness of Martin might parallel forgiving herself for falling into
the Gorgon system's traps.

Dumbledore (Derrida/Habermas) as a Guide:
How does Dumbledore guide Hannah in reconciling hospitality and the public sphere?

Dumbledore, as a fusion of Derrida and Habermas, helps Hannah understand the balance between hospitality
(openness to others) and the public sphere (responsibility to society). His role is to guide Hannah toward a
balance of these philosophies, helping her integrate the best of both without succumbing to Voldemort’s
ideology of no choice.

Snape (Foucault):
What role does Snape (Foucault) play?

Snape is a neutral observer, aware of the flaws in the Gorgon system but too entangled to act against it. He
represents the understanding of power and control, but he is not a key figure in the rebellion, merely someone
who sees through the system and occasionally hints at its weaknesses.

Voldemort’s Ideology of "No Choice" vs. Arendt’s Hospitality:
How do these philosophies clash?

Voldemort’s "no choice" represents a worldview of determinism and the idea that power and control are
inevitable. Arendt, on the other hand, believes in freedom and hospitality—the ability to choose ethical actions
even within oppressive systems. This ideological battle plays out in Hannah’s decisions, where she must
decide whether to use Voldemort’s tools for good or find a different path.
Personal Stakes for Martin the Zombie:
What are Martin’s stakes in the story?

Martin’s past relationship with the snakes—as their ruler and their abuser—ties into Hannah’s internal struggle.
He is significant not only because of his connection to Hannah but also as a symbol of the misuse of power
and the consequences of becoming a Gorgon.

The Gorgon Civil War:
How does the Gorgon civil war over genetic engineering escalate?

The civil war centers around genetic engineering, where Gorgons debate how these enhancements create
class divisions. The stakes of the war involve who controls genetic modification, with one side wanting to use it
for further domination while the other argues for its more restrained use.

The Hydra in the Gorgon System:
Backdrop of Globalized Power:

The Hydra represents the backdrop of the globalized neoliberal power system, enforcing control over all
institutions (education, military, judicial). It’s not a central enemy but a contextual force that defines the world
the characters navigate.

Hivemind Idioms and the Music Industry:
How do hivemind idioms in lyrics function?

The lyrics of music carry hivemind idioms, repeating messages that reinforce neoliberal subjectivity. People
who consume this music, unaware of its insidious effects, turn into Gorgons over time.

Is there resistance to the hivemind idioms?

Most people have already become Gorgons, but a few, like Hannah, begin to see the effects of the idioms and
resist. Those who resist are either ostracized or medicated with soma.

Neoliberal Psychologists and Soma:
How do psychologists planting snake eggs and soma prescriptions work together?

Neoliberal psychologists implant snake eggs (thought patterns) through psychological manipulation, while
psychiatrists prescribe soma to maintain mental sedation. Together, they keep society compliant and prevent
critical thinking. The conditioning is largely accepted by society, as they see it as necessary for stability.

Rebellion and Hannah’s Role:
What are the key steps in Hannah’s rebellion?

Hannah’s rebellion involves a gradual cutting off of her snakes. Her realization of the Eichmann trial and her
blame as a self-hating witch starts this process. She must navigate the psychological system, resist soma
prescriptions, and face the snake eggs implanted by society.

Do the Goths play a role in the rebellion?
The Goths, while passive, eventually join or influence the rebellion. Their unique ability to see Gorgons makes
them valuable allies, though they must be convinced to act.

What are the implications of Order 66?

The cancellation of jesters (comedians) during Order 66 marked the beginning of the Gorgon takeover. The
loss of these dissenting voices left a gap in resistance, but Hannah’s rebellion represents a new movement that
revives the spirit of resistance.

Arendtian Self-Sacrifice:
How does self-sacrifice manifest as a virtue in the Gorgon system?

Self-sacrifice in the Arendtian sense is a public, courageous act of responsibility. It involves putting one’s own
power or reputation on the line to serve the common good, which breaks the Gorgon cycle of self-preservation.
For Gorgons, this represents the ultimate moral awakening, as it means stepping away from their narcissistic
tendencies.

no lets go through absolutely everything ?
Alright, let’s go through everything from the ground up, based on the literal elements of the world you’ve built
so far:

1. Gorgons and Their Nature:
Literal Gorgons: They are creatures who refuse to introspect and avoid seeing themselves. Their snakes
represent the hivemind idioms implanted into society, and these snakes are literally invisible unless you have
special training to see them, like Goths. The Gorgons don’t know they’re Gorgons, which parallels how society
is blind to its own degrowth after passing the event horizon.

High-Tech, Low-Life Setting: Society has already passed the point of no return (as explained by degrowth
literature), but most people are unaware. Gorgons rule a cyberpunk dystopia where the neoliberal subjectivity
dominates.

2. Hannah (Arendt/Voldemort) and Duality:
Literal Duality: Hannah shifts between her Arendt and Voldemort sides depending on her moral choices. When
she chooses altruism and hospitality, she is Arendt. When she chooses to use manipulation and power
(Voldemort’s "no choice" ideology), she becomes Voldemort. The audience is never told this explicitly but
witnesses it as a literal transformation based on her actions.

Snakes: Hannah speaks Gorgon fluently but controls the snakes without ever letting one get on her head
(though occasionally they slip by without her knowing due to their complexity). Her mastery of the snakes is
central to her power as a witch.

3. Martin the Zombie (Heidegger):
Literal Zombie: Martin comes from Germany, representing an ideology that has lost to the Gorgons twice in
war. He is the king of the snake library, meaning he has deep knowledge of the snakes but uses it for evil
purposes. His connection to Hannah complicates her internal conflict between control and forgiveness.

The Black Books: Martin’s Black Books are literal texts that contain the philosophical basis for how Geeubeles
and others became mass snake charmers. These books are widely debated and controversial, representing
dangerous ideas that have shaped the world. They’re literal tools of power and manipulation, used to dominate
through the snakes.

4. Snakes and Hivemind Idioms:
Literal Snakes: The snakes represent repeated idioms that people pick up from music and culture, implanting
themselves invisibly onto people’s heads. The more they engage with these hivemind idioms, the more they
become Gorgons.

Snake Eggs: Neoliberal psychologists literally implant these eggs onto people’s heads, reinforcing neoliberal
subjectivity. Psychiatrists prescribe SOMA to keep everyone compliant and mentally subdued.

Martin and Snake Mastery: Martin’s knowledge of the snake library allows him to manipulate others without
falling victim to the snakes himself. Like Hannah, he has control over the snakes but uses them for evil.

5. The Hydra:
Backdrop of the Globalized Power System: The Hydra isn’t a literal enemy but represents the neoliberal global
empire that controls everything from education to the military. It’s always there in the background, shaping the
rules and reinforcing Gorgon control, but not the primary villain.
6. Dumbledore (Derrida/Habermas):
Literal Guide: Dumbledore represents the balance between hospitality (Derrida) and the public sphere
(Habermas). His role is to help Hannah navigate the philosophical challenges she faces between these two
ideologies. He’s indifferent to the snakes, like most non-evil characters, seeing them but not engaging deeply.

7. Snape (Foucault):
Literal Observer: Snape represents the complex relationship with power and control, understanding how the
Gorgon system works but remaining mostly entangled in it. He’s more of a neutral character, occasionally
offering insight into how the system controls people but not fully acting against it.

8. Zombies and Their Wars with Gorgons:
Literal Wars: The zombies from Germany fought the Gorgons twice and lost both times. This failure represents
the inability of philosophical thought (like Heidegger’s) to overcome the dominant Gorgon system. However,
zombies still exist in this world, and their undead nature means their ideas are never fully gone, always lurking
as a third ideological force.

9. The Eichmann Trial:
Literal Catalyst: The trial of Eichmann in Jerusalem is where Hannah is blamed for being a self-hating witch.
This trial is a pivotal moment in her redemption arc, where she begins to confront her own power as both a
witch and Voldemort. The trial also exposes the depths of her control over the snakes and how she used that
power.

10. Music Industry and Hivemind Idioms:
Literal Influence: The music industry literally plants hivemind idioms into society through lyrics. As people
repeat these lyrics, the snakes implant themselves, and society becomes more Gorgon-like. The music
industry is a tool used to perpetuate neoliberal control through repetition.

11. Soma:
Literal Drug: SOMA, prescribed by psychiatrists, keeps people mentally sedated and compliant. It is a key
component in maintaining Gorgon control over the population, dulling the mind and preventing rebellion.

12. Order 66 and the Cancellation of Jesters:
Literal Event: Order 66 marked the mass cancellation of jesters (comedians), which was one of the final steps
in the Gorgon takeover. The jesters represented the last line of defense against the hivemind, and their
cancellation removed the voice of dissent. The rebellion led by Hannah seeks to fill this void.

13. Hannah’s Rebellion:
Literal Cutting Off of Snakes: Hannah’s rebellion involves the literal act of cutting off her snakes, rejecting the
hivemind idioms and the Gorgon system. This act symbolizes her breaking free from the control that has
overtaken society. Her rebellion is triggered by the Eichmann trial, where she faces her own complicity in the
system and decides to resist.

14. Self-Sacrifice (Arendtian Virtue):
Literal Political Act: Self-sacrifice in this world is a literal public action, where a character puts their power and
status on the line to break the Gorgon cycle. This is how characters, including Gorgons, can reject the
system—by making a public, courageous stand for the common good.

15. The Role of Goths:
Literal Passive Observers: The Goths see everything happening but remain largely uninvolved unless directly
forced to act. They can see the snakes and Gorgons, but because they embrace all weirdness, they don’t see
an immediate problem with the growing number of snakes. Eventually, they may be drawn into the rebellion as
key allies.

clarify, or dive deeper!

review whole story and ask a bunch of questions

Here’s a review of the entire story so far, highlighting key points, relationships, and literal connections, followed
by questions that might help flesh things out further or tackle unresolved areas:
Core Concepts and World Setup:
Gorgons:

Literal creatures that refuse to introspect, their snakes are hivemind idioms implanted via music, psychology,
and societal norms. Most people don’t know they are Gorgons, much like society doesn’t realize it’s passed the
event horizon (degrowth is inevitable).

Order 66 intensified their presence, but they always had power. Post-Order 66, the doubling of snakes spread
further through culture and media.

Hannah (Arendt/Voldemort):

She embodies a literal moral duality: when making moral, altruistic choices, she is Hannah (Arendt); when
making destructive, self-serving decisions, she is Voldemort.

Hannah is a powerful witch who can control the snakes without letting them attach to her head, although they
can sometimes slip by. Her journey of redemption is triggered by the Eichmann trial, where she is blamed for
being a self-hating witch.

Snakes are complex, each with its own power level and ability, and Hannah must navigate their control,
avoiding becoming fully consumed like other Gorgons.

Martin the Zombie (Heidegger):

Martin is from Germany, tied to zombies who have fought the Gorgons twice and lost. He’s the king of the
snake library, using his knowledge of snakes for evil purposes.

His influence spread through his Black Books, which were responsible for creating figures like Geeubeles, the
mass snake charmers who manipulate society using snakes for zombie goals.

Dumbledore (Derrida/Habermas):

A fusion of Derrida’s hospitality and Habermas’ public sphere philosophy, Dumbledore is an indifferent figure
regarding the snakes. He’s a mentor to Hannah, guiding her to balance these philosophical tensions.

Snape (Foucault):

He’s a neutral observer who understands how power and control function in the Gorgon system but is
entangled within it. Snape’s role is one of awareness, but he remains caught up in the Gorgon structure.

Hydra (Global Power System):

The Hydra is the backdrop of the neoliberal order, controlling education, military, and judiciary systems. The
Hydra is growing thin due to the rise of Typhon (representing BRICS) in the South China Sea, signaling a
challenge to the established power.

The Typhoon (Typhon) is a literal representation of the BRICS alliance rising to disrupt the Hydra’s dominance.
Psychology and Soma:

Neoliberal psychologists implant snake eggs on people's heads, reinforcing control. At the same time,
psychiatrists prescribe SOMA, a drug that keeps people mentally sedated and compliant.

Music Industry and Hivemind Idioms:

The music industry spreads hivemind idioms through lyrics, which are repeated until people are gradually
transformed into Gorgons. Most people don’t resist the music’s influence.

Order 66 and Jesters:

Order 66 was when the jesters (comedians and dissenters) were cancelled, marking the intensification of
Gorgon power. With the jesters silenced, the Gorgon system was able to spread unchecked.

Goths:

The Goths are passive observers who can see the Gorgons and snakes but do not actively interfere. However,
they may become necessary allies in the rebellion if they decide to act.

Hannah’s Rebellion:

Her rebellion involves literally cutting off her snakes, symbolizing the rejection of the hivemind idioms and the
Gorgon control. This rebellion is sparked by her trial and internal conflict over her complicity in the system.

Questions:
Hannah’s Duality and Redemption:
What exactly happens in the trial of Eichmann that causes Hannah to see herself as a self-hating witch? Is
there a specific moment where she realizes her power as both Arendt and Voldemort?

How does Hannah’s relationship with Martin (Heidegger) evolve? Does she forgive him, or does he remain a
source of internal conflict? What’s the turning point for her regarding him?

What is Hannah’s ultimate goal in the rebellion—does she want to destroy the Gorgon system or reform it?
How does she see herself post-rebellion?

Martin the Zombie and the Black Books:
How do the Black Books continue to influence the world after the wars between zombies and Gorgons? Do
they serve as a manual for others to manipulate the snakes?

What role do Geeubeles and the mass snake charmers play in modern society? Have they been defeated, or
are they still influential in manipulating the populace?

Gorgon Society and the Hydra:
How does Typhon (BRICS) influence the Hydra and the Gorgon system? Is Typhon a force for good, simply a
rival power, or another form of control that could rival the Gorgons?

What exactly happens after Order 66? How does the doubling of snakes further affect society, and does the
power dynamic shift further toward total Gorgon domination?
Music Industry and Hivemind Idioms:
Is there any underground resistance against the music industry and its hivemind idioms? Are there alternative
forms of media or culture that try to fight back?

What happens to those who resist the hivemind idioms—are they ostracized, drugged with SOMA, or simply
silenced?

Snakes and Ideology:
How do snakes differ in their power levels? Are certain snakes more dangerous or influential than others?
Does the snake library that Martin controls hold untapped power that could be key to the rebellion?

How do the neoliberal psychologists and psychiatrists rationalize their role in society? Do they believe they are
doing what’s best, or are they fully aware of their role in reinforcing Gorgon control?

Rebellion and Self-Sacrifice:
What steps does Hannah take to literally cut off her snakes? Is this a physical process, or does it involve
rejecting the influence of specific ideologies?

How does self-sacrifice manifest in this world? Are there characters who publicly give up their status or power
to break the Gorgon cycle, or is Hannah the first to do so?

Do the Goths eventually join the rebellion? If so, what motivates them to finally act after remaining passive
observers for so long?

Order 66 and Jesters:
Is there any remnant of the jesters after Order 66, or were they completely silenced? Does the rebellion seek
to bring back this lost form of dissent and free speech?

How does the cancellation of jesters change the public’s view of humor and dissent? Is it seen as dangerous,
or do people forget the role jesters once played in keeping society in check?

These questions are aimed at exploring the depth of the story and clarifying how different elements interact
and affect the world. Let me know which parts you’d like to expand on or refine!

your getting too caught up in the details lets go through the whole arch here
Alright, let’s refocus and go through the whole arc of the story in a clear, overarching way:

1. The Gorgon World and Control:
Gorgons have always been in control. Their snakes, which represent hivemind idioms, spread through society
via music, media, and the internet. Over time, they have reinforced their power by making everything for sale,
from basic necessities to VPNs for those seeking alternative information.

Order 66 marked a point of intensification, when the number of snakes doubled, spreading even further
throughout society and silencing dissenters like the jesters (comedians and intellectuals). However, Gorgons
have always held power, and Order 66 just solidified their dominance.

2. Hannah’s Journey (Arendt/Voldemort):
Hannah is both Arendt and Voldemort, her form changing based on her moral choices. As Arendt, she
embraces altruism and seeks to use her power for good, but as Voldemort, she embodies self-interest and
manipulation, using the same tools as the Gorgons.

Her journey begins with the Eichmann trial, where she is blamed as a self-hating witch and confronts her role
in the Gorgon system. She can control the snakes, unlike most people, but must decide how to use that power.
The trial sparks her redemption arc, where she eventually seeks to lead a rebellion against the Gorgon order.

3. Martin the Zombie and the Black Books:
Martin the Zombie (Heidegger) played a critical role in providing the ideological framework that allowed figures
like Geeubeles to become mass snake charmers. His Black Books are controversial texts that contain the dark
intellectual underpinnings of the Gorgon system.

Martin is a powerful figure in Hannah’s life, and her relationship with him mirrors her internal conflict—whether
to forgive him and her past, or to break free entirely from the destructive ideologies he represents.

4. The Hydra and the Rise of Typhon (BRICS):
The Hydra represents the global neoliberal system that dominates the world. As the Gorgons' control spreads,
the Hydra grows thin, unable to maintain its dominance fully.

Typhon, representing BRICS, rises as a new force in the South China Sea to challenge the Hydra's
dominance. This signals a shift in global power, where the traditional structures are being disrupted by
emerging powers. Typhon is not inherently good or bad, but it represents a new wave of influence.

5. Goths, Zombies, and Information Control:
Goths and Zombies are among those most fed up with the Gorgon system. They try to bypass Gorgon control
using VPNs, but since everything is for sale in the Gorgon world, they are still playing by the rules of the
system.

Those shut out from the Gorgon-controlled internet seek alternative ways of communicating and sharing
information, though these efforts are constantly under threat of being co-opted or blocked by the Gorgons.

6. Hannah’s Rebellion:
Hannah’s rebellion centers around cutting off her own snakes, a symbolic and literal rejection of the Gorgon
system. As she begins her journey, she starts to gather others who have either resisted the Gorgons or are
seeking to break free from their control, including Goths, Zombies, and remnants of the jesters.
The rebellion is not just about destroying the Gorgon system but also about reforming society, offering an
alternative that breaks away from the hivemind idioms and the commodification of every aspect of life.

7. Conclusion and Final Confrontation:
The final act sees Hannah’s duality (Arendt vs. Voldemort) come into play in a decisive moment. Will she use
Gorgon tools to fight the system, risking becoming like them, or will she find a new way to lead the rebellion
based on hospitality and freedom?

The Hydra’s thinning and the rise of Typhon destabilize the global power balance, creating an opening for
Hannah’s rebellion. However, new threats emerge, and the final confrontation will determine whether the
Gorgon cycle can truly be broken.

Key Themes:
Moral Duality: Hannah’s journey is a constant struggle between moral and immoral choices, and her internal
conflict is mirrored by the external battle against the Gorgon system.

Power and Ideology: The role of Martin the Zombie and the Black Books underscores the danger of intellectual
power used for manipulation, and how easily ideas can be twisted to serve oppressive systems.

Rebellion and Redemption: The story revolves around Hannah’s redemption arc as she moves from complicity
in the system to leading a rebellion that challenges the very foundation of Gorgon control.

This is the full arc of the story. Would you like to expand on any key parts or refine some of the themes or
events?


give me possibilities for the soviet union as analogy

For the Soviet Union in this world, there are several mythological analogies or symbolic representations that
could align with its historical role, ideology, and eventual collapse. Here are a few possibilities:

1. The Titan Kronos (Cronus):
Why it fits: Kronos, the leader of the Titans, devoured his own children to prevent them from overthrowing him,
much like the Soviet Union's focus on controlling and suppressing dissent within its own ranks. Eventually,
Kronos is overthrown by Zeus and the Olympian gods, reflecting the fall of the Soviet Union as it was
overpowered by internal and external pressures.

In your world: Kronos could represent a once-powerful, authoritarian regime that sought to maintain control by
devouring or silencing potential threats. Its downfall symbolizes the inability to sustain power through paranoia
and repression, leading to its collapse.

2. Atlas:
Why it fits: Atlas was condemned to hold up the heavens for eternity, carrying the weight of the world on his
shoulders. The Soviet Union, in its efforts to maintain a vast empire and its global ideology (communism), took
on the immense burden of managing both domestic issues and its role in the Cold War.

In your world: Atlas could represent the Soviet Union’s collapse under the weight of its ambitions and
responsibilities. Its efforts to sustain its global power structure eventually caused it to crumble, symbolizing the
fall of a system burdened by its own expectations.
3. The Phoenix:
Why it fits: The Phoenix rises from its own ashes, symbolizing rebirth and regeneration. After the collapse of
the Soviet Union, Russia and other former Soviet states emerged, trying to rebuild themselves in new forms.

In your world: The Soviet Union could be represented by a Phoenix, which, after collapsing and burning, rises
again in new forms—such as the Russian Federation and the attempts to reclaim influence through new
means like BRICS. It’s a cyclical symbol of death and rebirth.

4. The Hydra's Severed Head:
Why it fits: The Hydra, with its multiple heads, can be seen as a symbol of global power systems. When one
head is cut off, another grows back. The collapse of the Soviet Union can be viewed as one head of the Hydra
being severed, but it didn’t lead to the end of global power struggles—other powers simply took its place.

In your world: The Soviet Union as one of the Hydra’s heads represents its role as a global superpower. Its
collapse gave rise to new global dynamics and the shifting balance of power, but the overall system of control
continued.

5. The Iron Giants (Talos):
Why it fits: In Greek mythology, Talos was a giant automaton made of bronze who protected Crete. He was
powerful but ultimately vulnerable and was destroyed through a weakness in his heel. The Soviet Union's great
strength in industrial and military might masked underlying weaknesses, leading to its sudden collapse.

In your world: The Soviet Union could be represented as an Iron Giant, symbolizing the might of its industrial
and military complex. However, like Talos, it had a fatal flaw (internal instability, economic troubles) that
eventually led to its downfall.

6. Prometheus:
Why it fits: Prometheus, who stole fire from the gods and gave it to humanity, can be seen as a revolutionary
figure. The Soviet Union, emerging from the Russian Revolution, sought to give the "fire" of communism to the
world. However, like Prometheus, it was punished for overreaching, and its ambitions were ultimately curtailed.

In your world: The Soviet Union as Prometheus represents the revolutionary spirit that sought to upend the
existing order, but in doing so, it faced constant punishment and ultimately fell, bound by the very forces it tried
to challenge.

7. Icarus:
Why it fits: Icarus, who flew too close to the sun, represents the Soviet Union’s overreach during the Cold War.
Its ambitious attempts to expand its influence, compete with the U.S., and sustain its vast empire led to its
eventual collapse, much like Icarus fell when his wings melted.

In your world: Icarus could symbolize the Soviet Union’s downfall due to its overambitious foreign policy,
economic overextension, and the strain of trying to maintain its status as a superpower.

Which of these possibilities resonates most with the role you envision for the Soviet Union in your story?

global power.

Key Characters and Themes:
Hannah (Arendt/Voldemort):
Hannah embodies both Arendt and Voldemort, her form shifting depending on her moral choices. When acting
with altruism and kindness, she is Arendt. When making self-serving, manipulative choices, she becomes
Voldemort.

Snakes represent the hivemind idioms that attach themselves to people’s heads. Hannah can control them
without letting them attach, although sometimes they slip by.

Redemption Arc: Her journey of cutting off her snakes symbolizes her rejection of the Gorgon system and her
role in the rebellion.

Martin the Zombie (Heidegger):
Martin is the king of the snake library, controlling knowledge about how ideologies (snakes) function and how to
manipulate people. His Black Books played a role in turning others, like Geeubeles, into mass snake charmers.

him and the ideologies he represents.

The Hydra (Neoliberal Order) and Typhon (BRICS):

creates a new dynamic in the global power struggle.

Goths and Zombies:
Goths and Zombies are outsiders who resist the Gorgon system but still have to engage with it, often by buying
VPNs to avoid Gorgon-controlled information networks.

Goths are passive, not actively engaging in battles, while Zombies represent a defeated ideology (Soviet) that
tries to regain influence, like in Ukraine.

Order 66 and the Jesters:
Order 66 marked the mass cancellation of jesters (intellectuals and comedians), allowing the Gorgon system to
become more powerful and control speech.

After Order 66, Gorgon influence doubled, and society became more entrenched in repetitive idioms.

Main Story Arc:
Gorgon Society and Control:

Society is dominated by Gorgon control, with different factions representing different ideologies. The internet is
controlled by Gorgon snakes, and people are shut out from information unless they pay for VPNs.

Hannah’s Rebellion:

Hannah’s journey starts with her realization during the Eichmann trial, where she’s accused of being a
self-hating witch. She begins to question her role in the Gorgon system and leads a rebellion to cut off the
snakes (ideologies).

Her internal struggle is represented by her dual nature—whether to use Voldemort’s destructive tactics or stick
to Arendtian ideals of hospitality and moral action.

Conflicts Between Gorgons:

Different Gorgons (neoliberal, Soviet, BRICS) fight for dominance, mirroring real-world power struggles.
Ukraine becomes a literal battleground between Mafia Bear Gorgon and Western Neoliberal Gorgons.

Reclamation of Power:

As the Hydra grows thin, the rise of Typhon (BRICS) disrupts the global order, and new battles between
ideologies emerge. Hannah’s rebellion challenges not just the Gorgon system but the very idea of control
through repeated idioms.

ideological idioms, making them more susceptible to Gorgonization.

The Gorgon Growth in the 70s:
Merger of Personal and Political: During the 1970s, the shift toward identity politics and the idea that the
personal is political allowed the Gorgons to grow more pervasive. Ideologies weren’t just things you believed
in; they became part of who you are. This alignment made it easier for Gorgonic ideologies to embed
themselves into everyday life and expand their control.

Neoliberal Expansion: In the same period, neoliberal ideologies took root, promoting the idea that everything is
for sale—including personal identity and political beliefs. This further blurred the lines between the personal
and the political, as individuals became commodified under neoliberal systems. Gorgons flourished in this
environment, where people’s thoughts were increasingly tied to economic and political structures.

Aging of the Gorgons: By the time we reach the present (or a future point in the story), the Gorgons have
grown old and entrenched, controlling all aspects of life through both economic and ideological control
systems. Their power has expanded from the 1970s onward, fueled by the weakening boundaries between
personal autonomy and political/ideological control.

what was the apple
? are there are alternative accounts of, theological, what exactly the apple are

Yes, there are alternative theological interpretations of what the apple (or the fruit from the Tree of Knowledge
of Good and Evil) represents in the Garden of Eden story. While the Bible doesn’t specifically say it was an
apple (the term “apple” came later in popular Christian tradition), the fruit has been interpreted in various ways
across different religious, philosophical, and theological traditions. Here are some alternative interpretations of
the apple/fruit:

1. The Fruit as Knowledge (of Good and Evil):
The most common interpretation is that the fruit represents knowledge, particularly the knowledge of good and
evil. By eating the fruit, Adam and Eve became aware of moral distinctions, which marked the end of their
innocence.

2. The Fruit as Temptation/Disobedience:
In some interpretations, the fruit symbolizes temptation or disobedience. It was not necessarily the fruit itself
but the act of defying God’s command that led to the fall. This view emphasizes the human tendency to stray
from divine will or authority.

3. The Fruit as Desire/Sensuality:
Some theological traditions interpret the fruit as a symbol of sensuality or desire. In this view, eating the fruit
represents the awakening of human passions and desires—particularly sexual desire—that were dormant in
the state of innocence.

4. Gnostic Interpretation:
In Gnostic Christianity, the fruit is sometimes seen as a positive symbol. It represents the gaining of wisdom
and the ability to see through the material world, understanding deeper truths about reality and the cosmos. In
some Gnostic readings, the serpent is even seen as a liberator who encouraged Eve to seek true knowledge.

5. The Fruit as Self-Awareness:
In certain philosophical readings, the fruit represents self-awareness or consciousness. Adam and Eve’s
decision to eat the fruit could be seen as the first step toward becoming fully autonomous beings, aware of
their own existence and moral agency, but also of their limitations and eventual mortality.
6. The Fruit as Ego:
Some modern interpretations, particularly in psychological and philosophical circles, see the fruit as a symbol
of the ego or self-importance. By eating it, humans shifted from a state of unity with creation to one where they
prioritized themselves and their own desires, leading to separation from divine harmony.

Questions for Development:
Which interpretation of the apple fits best with your story's Gorgon system? Could the fruit represent the
moment when people first became trapped in ideological repetition?

Is there a modern equivalent of the apple in your world—something that causes people to fall into Gorgon
control?

How do different groups in your world interpret the apple? Do the Zombies or Goths have different views on
what the fruit means in relation to their own ideology?

Would you like to explore a specific interpretation of the apple that connects directly to the themes of your
story?

only traps the Gorgons in a cycle of self-centeredness but also infects others, turning them into Gorgons as
well. By adopting the victimhood mentality, people become repetitive ideologues, spreading the same
dehumanizing ideas that reinforce the Gorgon system. The victimhood narrative feeds into a narcissistic loop
where everyone is caught in a cycle of self-victimization, thus perpetuating the Gorgon control.

Key Points:
Victimhood as Narcissism: The Gorgons use the ideology of victimhood as a justification for their own
narcissistic tendencies. By portraying themselves as victims, they avoid accountability, while manipulating
others into echoing their narrative.

Spreading the Gorgon Influence: Once others adopt the victimhood narrative, they too become Gorgons,
repeating the same ideological idioms and falling into the narcissistic cycle. The Gorgon system expands as
more people adopt the belief that they are victims, and this self-justifying mentality allows them to impose their
ideologies on others.
Narcissism Fuels Gorgon Expansion: As narcissistic groups form around these ideologies, they feed off one
another, creating a larger network of ideological echo chambers. These echo chambers reinforce the
dehumanizing, repetitive narratives that keep everyone trapped in the Gorgon system.

Here’s a breakdown of your points and how they fit together:

Key Concepts:
Gorgon Victim Ideology and Its Spread:
Disenchantment of the World: The world has been disenchanted—people no longer believe in gods or higher
spiritual truths, and the market (as Foucault describes) has become the only system of veridiction. In this
context, neoliberalism emerges as the only "truth," pushing other belief systems aside and allowing victim
ideologies to spread easily.

Realization of Being a Gorgon:

Individuals realize they are Gorgons when they become aware that their thoughts are not their own—when
they recognize that they’ve been repeating hivemind idioms, or when they distinguish their own ruminations
from the ruminations of others. This self-awareness brings the realization of being trapped in Gorgon control.

Jesters and Uncomfortable Truths:

Jesters are crucial because they force people to confront uncomfortable truths through questions and jokes.
They disrupt the repetitive, comfortable narrative of the Gorgons and challenge people to think critically,
breaking the cycle of victimhood.

Groupthink and Narcissism:

The Gorgon system thrives on groupthink and agreeableness. People are told to check their privilege, but in
reality, this serves to elevate the biggest narcissists to positions of power, as they manipulate these narratives
to their advantage, consolidating Gorgon control.

Those Who Stay Human:

There are those who never become Gorgons. These individuals are resistant to the victimhood ideologies and
groupthink, and they manage to stay human by maintaining their individual thoughts and rejecting the
ideological repetition.

ok read this as the backdrop of why:

Carrying Over the Burdens of Trace:
Weaving together Civic Rituals
on the Silk Roads of the Post-Apocalypse
Saul Nassau
December 2019
Perhaps We Are All Ritual Practitioners 1
Gut Feelings, Enclosed Landscapes 10
Same Crises, Di

The disenchantment led to neoliberalism being perceived as the only valid system because, unlike traditional
religious or spiritual systems, neoliberal capitalism offers a measurable return on investment (ROI). In spiritual
systems, praying to a god or following religious rituals may not guarantee a tangible outcome or reward,
whereas engaging in capitalist rituals (working, investing, buying, selling) often yields immediate, visible
returns—money, success, or material goods. This focus on measurable outcomes makes neoliberalism feel
more reliable and true to people living in a disenchanted world where the concept of faith or belief in unseen
forces has weakened.

Key Elements of Neoliberalism:
Return on Investment: In a neoliberal system, effort is directly linked to a tangible result (financial success or
material gains). This direct feedback loop makes it easier to adopt and repeat neoliberal ideologies because
people feel they are getting something in return.

Quantifiable Success: Neoliberalism measures success in quantifiable terms (profit, GDP, efficiency), which
contrasts with the abstract or non-quantifiable benefits of religious or spiritual practices (faith, enlightenment,
salvation).

Individual Empowerment Myth: Neoliberalism promotes the idea that through personal effort and
entrepreneurship, anyone can succeed. This message, though often deceptive, resonates with people because
it offers a sense of control and self-empowerment.

Absence of Spiritual Forces and Vulnerability to Victimhood Ideologies:
The absence of belief in higher spiritual forces leaves people more vulnerable to victimhood ideologies
because, without an external anchor for meaning or purpose, individuals are more likely to cling to ideologies
that offer an explanation for their suffering. In the absence of spiritual frameworks, people are drawn to
narratives that explain their experiences of injustice, powerlessness, or disenfranchisement in terms of
systemic oppression, allowing them to see themselves as victims.

Without a belief in a higher purpose or a greater cosmic force to endure hardship for, victimhood ideologies
provide a sense of identity and community, while offering an explanation for personal struggles. This creates a
cycle where victimhood becomes the default narrative people turn to for understanding their place in a
disenchanted, neoliberal world.

Backdrop: "Carrying Over the Burdens of Trace":
Your reference to “Carrying Over the Burdens of Trace” touches on the idea that we are all ritual practitioners,
whether we realize it or not. In the post-apocalyptic or post-disenchanted world, civic rituals (like capitalist
rituals) take the place of religious or spiritual ones, weaving together fragmented societal practices to maintain
a sense of order and meaning. These rituals, like gut feelings or instinctive behaviors, become enclosed
landscapes where people repeat patterns that connect them to a larger economic or social system rather than
to a spiritual or transcendent one.

In this context, the civic rituals of neoliberalism—working, buying, investing—become secular replacements for
spiritual rituals. They provide a sense of purpose and stability in a world where traditional anchors of meaning
have dissolved.

Follow-up Questions:

religious ones, just with a capitalist twist?

How does the idea of ROI (return on investment) change the way people view their actions? Do they see
everything in terms of cost and benefit, even in personal relationships?
Do some people resist neoliberal rituals, clinging to remnants of older spiritual practices, or is the Gorgon
control of ideology so strong that these rituals are all-consuming?

How do jesters disrupt these neoliberal rituals and push people to question whether the ROI system is really
delivering what they seek?

Would you like to explore how the rituals of neoliberalism play out in your story, or how characters navigate this
disenchanted world?

read this as backdrop to this whole project

The text discusses how capitalism, colonialism, and trauma are intertwined, resulting in various contemporary
crises like environmental collapse, addiction, and collective trauma. These crises are linked to neoliberalism,
which has become a universal system, permeating all aspects of society and dominating civic life. The text also
examines rituals as central to daily life and considers how these rituals shape subjectivities, mediated by global
finance and cultural norms. Rituals are omnipresent, guiding behaviors and interactions, reinforcing both
individual and collective identities.

In the context of Gorgon victim ideology, this backdrop helps illustrate how neoliberalism, as a universal
system, propagates and colonizes not only economies but also minds. People fall into ideological patterns,
repeating idioms and participating in rituals without questioning the larger system. Victim ideologies, rooted in
neoliberalism's logic of market veridiction, spread through these rituals, as individuals ruminate over ideas and
expressions that reinforce their status as victims within the system.

The disenchantment of the world — the loss of belief in gods or higher spiritual forces — leaves individuals
vulnerable to adopting ideologies that serve as coping mechanisms for their existential anxieties. This is where
neoliberalism's dominance becomes crucial, as it offers tangible returns on investment (through market
participation) compared to religious or spiritual systems. The absence of higher beliefs allows victimhood
ideologies to flourish, as neoliberalism provides the framework in which grievances can be commodified and
repeated.

The Gorgon system, with its emphasis on repetition, idioms, and victim ideologies, fits well into this neoliberal
framework. Individuals become trapped in these cycles, reinforcing the power structures that perpetuate
neoliberalism and its ideologies. Jesters and other figures who challenge this status quo force people to
confront uncomfortable truths, much like the ritualistic behaviors discussed in the text.

How does the disenchantment of the world leave individuals more susceptible to adopting Gorgon-like

so we said between all the religions, religions deal with each other through capitalism right? that means
capitalism is the only universal language

Yes, exactly! The disenchantment of the world, in which traditional religious or spiritual beliefs lose their hold on
society, leaves a vacuum that is filled by capitalism as the only universal language. Since capitalism becomes
the dominant system of veridiction (as Foucault says), it acts as the framework through which all
ideologies—including religions—must engage with each other. As a result, people become more susceptible to
Gorgon-like ideologies because capitalism structures all human interactions and thought processes.

How Disenchantment and Capitalism Make People Vulnerable to Gorgon Ideologies:
Capitalism as the Universal Language: When religions and other belief systems must negotiate and compete
through capitalism, the spiritual or moral basis of these systems becomes secondary to their market value.
People start to view their beliefs and identities through the lens of capitalist logic, focusing on returns on
investment rather than deeper spiritual or existential meaning.

Absence of Transcendent Meaning: Without a belief in higher spiritual forces, individuals are left without a
larger purpose or guiding principle, making them more prone to adopting ideologies that offer a simplistic
explanation for their suffering. This creates an opening for victimhood ideologies and other Gorgon-like
systems, which provide people with a sense of identity and validation within the capitalist framework.

Market as Veridiction: The fact that capitalism becomes the universal system of truth-making means that
ideologies must be commodified and repeated in order to survive and thrive. This repetition turns ideas into
hivemind idioms that spread like Gorgon snakes, feeding on the logic of the market to gain traction and power.

Commodification of Ideologies: In a disenchanted world, ideologies themselves become commodified.
Victimhood ideologies, for instance, gain power because they can be packaged, sold, and repeated through
capitalist systems. This turns individuals into consumers and producers of ideologies, much like products in a
marketplace, leading to their entrapment in the Gorgon system.

Lack of a Moral Anchor: With no moral or spiritual anchor, individuals become dependent on social validation
through capitalist structures, reinforcing the narcissism and groupthink that fuel the Gorgon system. The lack of
an external moral authority means that people rely on the echo chambers of the market-driven world to define
their beliefs, which makes them more prone to adopting Gorgon-like repetitive ideologies.
Questions for Development:

with each other in the Gorgon system?

Gorgon-like cycles of repetition?

How do people without a spiritual or moral anchor find identity in Gorgon ideologies, and what prevents them
from breaking free from these repetitive cycles?

What role do neoliberal civic rituals play in reinforcing Gorgon-like ideologies, particularly in a disenchanted
world where capitalism is the only truth system?

Would you like to explore how this plays out in specific characters' journeys or focus more on the broader
societal dynamics of the Gorgon system?

New Characters and Concepts:
Pig Gorgons:
Characteristics: These are people who mock intellectualism and believe that reading Hegel makes one a
"himbo." They trivialize deeper thought and reduce philosophical discourse to superficial stereotypes. Their role
in the Gorgon system is to distract and diminish intellectual engagement, reinforcing shallow, repetitive
ideologies.

Nimrods:

Characteristics: Individuals with magical abilities who, upon encountering Gorgons, purposefully turn their
heads to stone. This represents their decision to consciously repeat hivemind idioms, knowing they are trapped
but choosing not to break free. The head stone symbolizes their complicity and deliberate participation in the
system.

#NoChoice Philosophy: They embody the philosophy of #nochoice, where people know they are repeating
harmful ideologies but feel compelled to do so for societal advantages. Nimrods are aware of the Gorgon
system but lack the will to resist it, seeing their complicity as a necessary evil.

Allegiance: Nimrods don’t have specific allegiances; their ability to “destone” themselves means they could
potentially shift sides, but they often remain neutral or self-serving.

The Citadel (Terracotta Warriors):

Characteristics: A group of warriors fully turned to stone, representing those who have completely internalized
the #nochoice philosophy. They view themselves as an army against the Gorgons, but their approach is
equally destructive. Unlike Nimrods, their entire body is stone, symbolizing complete ideological rigidity.

Role: They are active fighters against the Gorgons but are just as destructive as the Gorgons themselves,
showing how resistance can sometimes mirror the same oppressive patterns it aims to fight against.
