Research completed in 12m · 18 sources
#gorgonwars: Core Narrative, Characters, and Symbolism
Narrative Structure, Themes & Allegorical Elements
#gorgonwars is presented as an allegorical narrative that reframes contemporary intellectual and cultural
conflicts in mythological terms. At its core is the idea that direct discourse about certain truths has become
perilous or futile in the current climate – “we cannot name things directly, we must use mythology”
. The storyline thus unfolds in a symbolic landscape drawn from classic myths and philosophy. For example, it
invokes Plato’s Cave to illustrate society’s state of illusion: most people are like prisoners mistaking shadows
for reality and may even “react violently” or retreat when confronted with the light of truth
. The protagonist (often modeled on a figure like Hannah) escapes this cave of illusions and enters a
Nietzschean desert – a metaphor for the bleak, disorienting period after one abandons old certainties.
Nietzsche’s desert symbolizes the growth of nihilism and spiritual desolation (“The desert grows: woe to him
who harbors deserts!”
), but it is also a necessary stage for self-discovery and the creation of new values. Traveling through this
desert of isolation and doubt, the characters in #gorgonwars grapple with monstrous representations of
modern problems. The very name evokes the Gorgon of Greek myth (Medusa and her sisters): in the narrative,
“gorgons” represent people overrun by snake-like “hivemind” idioms – viral slogans or ideas they have not
digested themselves
. The “wars” refer to the endless battles of rhetoric and opinion online, where opposing sides sling petrifying
words at each other without understanding. Key themes include the struggle between individual critical thought
and collective ignorance, and the challenge of communicating truth in a distorted information landscape. The
story often emphasizes that confronting certain horrific realities head-on can be as deadly as looking into
Medusa’s eyes (which in myth would turn one to stone). Thus, like Perseus, one must approach indirectly –
using reflection and allegory as a shield – to slay the monster without succumbing to it
. In #gorgonwars, mythology itself becomes that reflective shield, a tool to reveal truths obliquely and bypass
the “petrifying” gaze of censors, algorithms, or dogmatic outrage. Under this allegorical structure, the tone is
often satirical and critical. The narrative exposes how modern discourse has been debased: many debates are
just people “debating with one another using non-digested ideas…‘empty chatter’ – mindless slogans” that they
repeat like an earworm, essentially functioning as snakes on a Gorgon’s head
. By weaving these critiques into mythic story arcs, #gorgonwars blends dark humor with social commentary. It
transforms abstract concepts (like neoliberalism, intellectual complacency, or online censorship) into concrete
fantastical images and plot points. This approach creates an accessible yet multi-layered commentary. On the
surface, it’s a fantastical saga of witches, gorgons and warriors; on a deeper level, it’s an allegory about our
“hivemind idioms era”
, calling readers to undertake the “actual hard work needed to become a subject” (i.e. a thinking individual)
rather than remain stone-like devotees of prevailing narratives
. Overall, the narrative structure follows a kind of hero’s journey of the intellect – from ignorance (the cave)
through a gauntlet of trials (the desert, battles with monsters) toward enlightenment and genuine community.
The use of allegory ensures that these heavy themes (personal awakening, collective trauma, resistance to
ideological oppression) remain engaging and universal – the story can be read and developed further without
being tied to one time or place, which is intentional. In fact, #gorgonwars deliberately cultivates a “mythological
lexicon” of its own, so that it can discuss sensitive truths in a subversive, unbannable way within controlled
digital spaces
. This rich mythopoetic framework is the backbone of #gorgonwars, enabling it to critique the present while
remaining timeless and open-ended.
Key Characters and Archetypes
The #gorgonwars mythos features a cast of characters who are less individual people than archetypes, each
embodying certain roles or attitudes in the grand allegory. Below are the key figures and what they represent:
Hannah – The protagonist or wise guide figure, inspired by political theorist Hannah Arendt. Hannah stands for
critical thought, moral courage, and the humanist values needed to resist the “gorgonizing” of society. She
often guides others out of Plato’s cave or through the desert, emphasizing understanding over ideology. (The
choice of name alludes to Arendt’s emphasis on thinking and judging for oneself, as well as her commitment to
human plurality and civic responsibility.)
Martin the Zombie – A philosophically charged character likely named after Martin Heidegger. “Martin”
shambles through the story as an undead sage whose ideas on “idle talk” (Gerede) haunt the discourse
. As a zombie, he symbolizes how profound ideas can be co-opted and mindlessly repeated after their
originator is “intellectually dead.” Martin the Zombie reminds us that without genuine understanding, even
philosophy can become lifeless jargon – a critique aimed at those who quote thinkers like Heidegger or
Foucault without real insight (the “empty chatter” that Heidegger warned about
). Despite his undead state, Martin occasionally offers grumbling commentary on the hollow discourse around
him, a nod to how Heidegger’s concepts still lurk in discussions of modern technology and society, though
often misunderstood.
Gorgons (The Snake-Heads) – The gorgons are not one single character but the masses of people ensnared
by hive-mind thinking. In #gorgonwars anyone who merely regurgitates propaganda or trendy jargon without
reflection is metaphorically a “gorgon” with snakes for hair. Each snake is a hivemind idiom – a catchphrase or
meme-ified idea that has taken on a life of its own in their mind
. Gorgons spread these ideas (snakes) to bite others, and their gaze is dangerous: engaging them directly in
argument can get one “petrified” (frozen out, cancelled, overwhelmed). Thus gorgons represent the peers and
pundits in our world who weaponize repeated slogans, often unconsciously. Part of the narrative’s conflict is
that even well-intentioned people (heroes-in-training) realize at some point “I was a gorgon participating in
#gorgonwars when I was just repeating empty slogans”
 – a moment of self-awareness that begins their journey to independent thought. Defeating the gorgon
influence requires the indirect methods and self-education that the story champions.
Goth Cops – A fanciful hybrid of subculture and authority, “goth cops” in the story are the guardians of the
status quo with a countercultural flair. They dress in edgy goth attire (perhaps to seem “alternative”), but in
function they are enforcers – the thought-police or moderators ensuring no one strays from approved
narratives. Goth cops symbolize the paradox of modern censorship: officials or algorithms that don a “cool” or
progressive appearance while patrolling speech and behavior. They might speak the language of mental health
or inclusivity but use it to shut down dissent – hence cops in goth clothing. In the narrative, Goth Cops are
obstacles the heroes encounter at the boundaries of the cave or the entrances to the citadel (see Citadel
Warriors below), issuing tickets or bans for “heresy.” They allegorically critique how corporate and state power
can mask itself as youthful rebellion or “for your own good” guidance even as it curtails freedom.
Jesters – The Jesters are the tricksters and truth-tellers of #gorgonwars. Dressed as clowns or fools, they have
license to mock everyone – gorgons, cops, even the heroes. In medieval tradition, the jester could speak truth
to the king without losing his head. Here, they represent the role of satire and meme culture in the internet age.
Jesters in the story might diffuse tense battles with absurd humor or riddles. Sometimes their japes contain
piercing insight that cuts through the fog of propaganda. They remind the protagonists not to take ideological
posturing too seriously – after all, laughter can break the hypnotic hold of demagogues. However, Jesters are
chaotic neutral; their jokes can just as easily sow confusion as clarity. This character highlights the ambivalent
power of humor and irony in our discourse – a tool that can undermine authority and groupthink, or conversely,
descend into nihilistic “clowning” that makes meaningful dialogue impossible.
Trolls – Hailing from the darker corners under the bridge, the Trolls of #gorgonwars epitomize malicious
mischief and provocation. These characters are drawn from both internet terminology and fairy-tale monsters.
In the narrative, trolls lurk on discussion threads and at the edges of battles, hurling insults, derailing
conversations, and feeding on conflict. They might not fight for any particular side; their goal is to undermine
morale and keep the war going for war’s sake. Trolls represent the phenomenon of online harassment and
bad-faith argument – those who engage not out of conviction but for “the lulz” or out of cruelty. The heroes
learn not to “feed the trolls,” as responding to them only spawns more chaos. As creatures, they are depicted
as gnarled, stony beings (often turning to stone in sunlight, in line with myth), symbolizing ignorance and
negativity that cannot survive the light of reason or empathy. The narrative uses Trolls to show how some
opposition one faces isn’t sincere disagreement but a destructive force that needs to be outwitted or ignored.
Nimrods – The Nimrods in #gorgonwars are ambitious builder figures who tragically sow discord. The name
comes from the biblical Nimrod, legendary for building the Tower of Babel which led to the confusion of
languages
. In the story, a Nimrod is someone (or a group) attempting grand projects of unity or truth – but doing so in a
hubristic or simplistic way that backfires. For example, a tech mogul trying to “connect the world” on one social
platform could be portrayed as a Nimrod: intending to unite people, but ending up creating Babel-like
fragmentation and misunderstanding. Nimrods thus stand for misguided intellectual or political movements that
seek universal solutions (a single tower of knowledge, one-size-fits-all ideology) and inadvertently shatter
communication. They might appear as tower-building engineers or evangelists speaking one “true language”
that others don’t understand. The result of a Nimrod’s work in the narrative is often a Wormhole or a babel of
tongues – some catastrophe that scatters people into isolated camps. In a more colloquial sense, “nimrod” can
mean an inept person; #gorgonwars plays on both meanings, portraying them as well-intentioned fools. They
are cautionary figures reminding the heroes that arrogance in knowledge or politics can create enduring chaos.
Citadel Warriors – These characters defend the Citadel – a metaphorical stronghold of entrenched power or
dogma. The Citadel could be an “ivory tower” of academia, a corporate headquarters, or an ideological
echo-chamber. Its warriors are the elite soldiers or gatekeepers who enforce the citadel’s doctrines and fend off
any outside challenges. They might be depicted as shining knights or faceless armored suits, representing
institutional authority. For instance, a Citadel Warrior could personify a hardened pundit or tenured gatekeeper
dismissing any new ideas that threaten the established paradigm. They are more disciplined and formidable
than the roving Goth Cops; if a protagonist tries to storm the Citadel (i.e. directly challenge prevailing
orthodoxy), they will face these warriors in pitched battle. Allegorically, Citadel Warriors stand for the status
quo’s intellectual enforcers – those deeply invested in maintaining the current system (whether that is
neoliberal technocracy, academic consensus, etc.). They clash with Nimrods (who try to build new towers) and
with the heroes (who seek to open the gates for change). The narrative uses these warriors to explore the
difficulty of dislodging deeply fortified beliefs and the conflict between innovative thought and established
“truths.”
Hegelian E-Girls – A strikingly modern archetype, the Hegelian e-girls are drawn from internet culture. They
are young women on social media who brand themselves with philosophical aesthetics – posting about Hegel,
dialectics, etc. – but whose depth of understanding is questionable. In #gorgonwars, they appear as
sorceress-like figures convening in covens or “councils,” charming audiences with high-sounding ideas that
might be more style than substance. The inclusion of Hegelian e-girls satirizes the trend of theory-as-persona:
it’s noted that many such self-proclaimed thinkers have not actually read Hegel at all
. Within the story, a Hegelian e-girl might recite half-understood aphorisms from a glowing grimoire (social
feed) to appear erudite, possibly leading followers astray with misinterpreted philosophy. They could even
accidentally summon intellectual “spirits” they can’t control (a sly nod to invoking Hegel’s Zeitgeist without
grasping it). A notable plot point involves an e-girl character – referred to as “Anna K. Winters” in the extended
lore – who attempts to appropriate the work of the main intellectual (a figure named Saul Nassau in the lore,
i.e. the thesis author) and in a burst of performative fervor calls him a fascist, sparking drama. This echoes
real-world instances of superficial radicalism and clout-chasing. In essence, Hegelian e-girls personify a
commodification of intellectualism: the reduction of complex ideas to trendy identity markers. They are both
alluring and dangerous in the narrative – at times helping to popularize mythic ideas, but also threatening to
dilute or distort them. The heroes must discern substance from flash when dealing with these characters.
(Despite the name, this archetype isn’t a gendered critique per se, but rather commentary on online intellectual
cultures; the “e-girl” label roots it in the phenomenon of internet personas.)
Others – The world of #gorgonwars is expansive, and several other minor characters/archetypes appear.
Witches, for instance, are mentioned as figures of healing and knowledge – “witch healing is the process of
untangling gorgon hivemind idioms… We can only heal collectively,” one snippet says, casting witches as
those working to undo curses of mass thought
. They likely represent marginal but wise voices (perhaps akin to therapists or communal elders) who practice
“magic” – i.e. unorthodox methods – to cure society’s ills. Egregores (collective thought-entities) and their
battles (#egregorewars) also loom in the background, reinforcing the theme of group minds at war. While not
individually listed, the presence of these and other mythic figures (Hydra heads, prophetic oracles,
wormhole-making “engineers,” etc.) enriches the narrative tapestry. Each character in #gorgonwars serves as
an allegorical device, sharply caricaturing some aspect of real-world ideology, behavior, or power structure.
Consistently, the characters maintain a double existence: they are at once fantastical beings in a story and
mirrors held up to facets of contemporary life (academic pretentiousness, internet trolling, state censorship,
grassroots resistance, and so on). This cast ensures that as the #gorgonwars saga continues to develop, it has
a ready palette of personae to draw on for illustrating new chapters of the cultural critique.
Mythological & Philosophical Symbolism
The #gorgonwars lexicon is rich with symbols drawn from mythology and philosophy. These symbols carry
allegorical meaning within the narrative:
Plato’s Cave: The allegory of the cave is foundational to #gorgonwars. It symbolizes the state of illusion and
ignorance in which many people exist. In the cave, prisoners watch shadows on the wall, mistaking them for
reality
. This represents how media narratives, propaganda, or one’s limited perspective can create a “false vision of
the truth, an illusion about reality”
. Leaving the cave (as the protagonist Hannah does) means breaking free of these illusions – but as Plato
noted, the sudden light of truth can be blinding and unwelcome. #gorgonwars uses the Cave to frame its
beginning: society at large is in chains, seeing only shadows (constructed images, ideological dogmas) and
often “would rather retreat back into the cave” than face uncomfortable truths
. Thus, the cave stands for the comfort of ignorance and the initial barrier that must be overcome for any real
change. It also sets up the theme that enlightenment is a difficult journey – one that demands questioning
one’s basic assumptions (akin to the painful process of turning around to see the fire and climbing out of the
cave).
Nietzschean Desert: After escaping the cave, the seeker in #gorgonwars finds themselves in a vast desert –
this evokes Nietzsche’s idea of the desert as the existential void one faces when old values have crumbled.
Nietzsche wrote, “The desert grows: woe to him who harbors deserts within”, referring to the spread of nihilism
and the inner emptiness that can arise in modern humanity
. In the story, the “Nietzschean desert” symbolizes a stage of loneliness, doubt, and trial. It’s the spiritual
wilderness where previous certainties (the cave’s shadows) are absent, and one must confront the possibility
that life has no inherent meaning except that which we create. This desert imagery underscores the theme of
nihilism that accompanies the breakdown of traditional beliefs (whether religious, or the collapse of trust in
institutions in a neoliberal age). However, it’s also in the desert that prophets and heroes often find insight.
#gorgonwars uses the desert to represent both the bleakness and opportunity of this stage: freed from illusion
but not yet at a new truth, one might wander “where no values hold, under a burning sun of skepticism.” It is
analogous to the period of deconstruction in intellectual awakening – dangerous because “withering of vital
forces” can occur if one stays too long in despair
, but it also hardens and prepares the hero for creative revaluation. In practical terms, this symbol speaks to
the experience of many who wake up to societal lies or personal disillusionment: there is a phase of alienation
and purposelessness (the desert) that one must survive to find a new guiding principle.
Snakes as Hivemind Idioms: Snakes – specifically the writhing snakes on Medusa’s head – are a central motif
in #gorgonwars. They represent viral ideas, memes, and clichés that spread through minds collectively. The
Urban Dictionary definition (written by the project’s creator) explicitly equates an “earworm” – a catchy tune one
can’t get out of one’s head – to a “snake on top of our gorgon heads”
. In other words, certain phrases or concepts take hold of people’s thinking in exactly this way. These hivemind
idioms include things like political slogans, buzzwords, or prevailing social media sentiments that individuals
adopt and repeat without critical thought. Each snake has a life of its own, but they often move in coordinated
ways (a hive mind of serpents). The more snakes someone has “in their hair,” the less independent their
thought – their perspective is essentially a product of the collective, not personal reasoning. In the story,
whenever a character succumbs to speaking in pre-packaged lines (for instance, an activist only quoting
therapy jargon, or a reactionary spouting talk-radio tropes), a snake is figuratively seen sprouting from their
head. This visualizes the concept of being possessed by the zeitgeist. The ultimate gorgon, Medusa, would be
someone so overrun by hivemind idioms that their gaze petrifies – meaning their presence stops all meaningful
dialogue and turns people rigid, because nothing genuine can get through the tangle of reflexive slogans. The
heroes aim to “untangle” and slay these snakes, which means fostering genuine, reflective language in place
of hollow catchphrases. The snake motif also connects to the idea of language as both weapon and parasite –
just as Medusa’s snakes were once living creatures co-opted onto her head, many of our thoughts are living
ideas introduced from external sources. #gorgonwars portrays reclaiming one’s mind from these intruders as a
heroic, painful process (often requiring that metaphorical “year of sitting and thinking and digesting thoughts” to
purge the snakes
).
The Hydra: The Hydra, a serpentine monster from Greek myth, is another potent symbol used in #gorgonwars
to represent problems that multiply when attacked directly. In mythology, the Hydra grew two heads back for
each one cut off
. Within the narrative, the Hydra stands for certain entrenched systems or ideologies – for example, neoliberal
capitalism, institutional racism, or online disinformation – that seem to regenerate in new forms whenever you
try to eliminate them. Cut down one corrupt official, two more rise; debunk one conspiracy theory, and two
more variants spring up. The Hydra’s regenerative property makes it a metaphor for the frustration of fighting
symptoms, not the root. Heroes facing the Hydra in #gorgonwars learn that they must cauterize the stumps (in
myth, Hercules used fire with his nephew’s help to prevent regrowth
). Allegorically, that means addressing underlying causes and using creative or unorthodox means, not just
brute force or straightforward debate. The Hydra also evokes the idea of a multi-headed enemy: in the context
of the story, it might be an alliance of troll armies, or a corporation with many divisions, or a propaganda
network with many outlets. Each head might speak a different narrative, but they’re part of the same beast.
This symbol reinforces the theme that the heroes are up against systemic, self-perpetuating adversaries – a
single decapitation strike won’t suffice; a more holistic or clever strategy (perhaps burning the neck, i.e.
fundamentally changing the conditions) is needed to truly vanquish the monster.
Wormhole: In #gorgonwars, the Wormhole symbolizes a tear in the fabric of reality or discourse – a shortcut, a
portal, or a collapse of distance. Wormholes in science fiction connect two far-apart points in space; in the
narrative, a wormhole can represent a sudden connection between disparate ideas or worlds, often bypassing
the normal barriers. This can be interpreted in a few ways. One, it may stand for the experience of falling down
an internet “rabbit hole”, where one click leads rapidly to another and suddenly a person is in a totally different
ideological space (e.g. someone watches a video and is algorithmically tunnelled into an extremist community
– essentially traveling through a wormhole of content). Thus, wormholes can be dangerous traps engineered
by the Nimrods or adversaries, dumping unwary travelers into bizarre echo chambers or alternate realities. On
the other hand, wormholes might also serve the heroes as secret passages to outmaneuver the enemy. In a
positive sense, a wormhole could be a hidden channel of communication that connects allies separated by vast
“distances” (cultural or geographical) without being detected – much like the contentinternet strategy creates a
subversive language that jumps past censors. The symbol of the wormhole therefore encapsulates the
collapse of space, time, or logical distance in the modern world. It’s an acknowledgment of how the internet era
creates instantaneous connections (and collisions) between very different people and ideas. A “wormhole”
event in the plot might be, for instance, when a minor forum discussion suddenly becomes globally viral
(connecting a cave to the wider world abruptly), or when a character uses arcane knowledge to teleport into
the Citadel. Thematically, it highlights unpredictability and the need for agility – heroes must be careful of
sudden shifts (they might emerge from a wormhole into an unexpected environment) but also can exploit them
to surprise their foes. In summary, wormholes represent the unstable pathways of the information age and the
nonlinear leaps in understanding (or mis-understanding) that myth-making and internet connectivity alike can
produce.
Each of these symbols – and others like mirrors (for reflection and indirect seeing), stone (for paralysis or
unchanging thought), fire (for knowledge or destruction), etc. – form a language that #gorgonwars uses to
communicate its deeper commentary. Because the project explicitly aims to create a “mythological lexicon”
, these symbols are consistently employed to encode ideas. This makes the content layered: one can read it as
fantasy, but those familiar with Plato, Nietzsche, or Greek myths will recognize the additional meaning. The
allegorical symbols ensure the project stays allegiant to big-picture ideas (enlightenment, nihilism, collectivism,
resilience, escapism) while telling an engaging story. Crucially, these mythic and philosophical references are
polymorphous – they can shift in interpretation as needed (for instance, a snake might mean deceit in one
chapter, and collective anger in another, but the overall idea of a dangerous uncontrollable idea-thread
remains). This flexibility is part of the “polymorphous mythology” the project espouses, allowing the symbolism
to evolve with future developments.
Historical & Cultural References
#gorgonwars draws on various real historical and cultural touchstones to enrich its allegory. These references
situate the mythic narrative in relation to actual history and ideas:
Artemisia Gentileschi: Artemisia is invoked as an icon of turning trauma into art and truth-telling. She was a
17th-century Italian Baroque painter who survived rape and public trial, then famously painted powerful images
of women exacting justice (Judith beheading Holofernes, etc.)
. In #gorgonwars, Artemisia symbolizes the transformative power of storytelling and myth-making to process
trauma. Just as Artemisia “painted plenty of paintings of strong and suffering women from mythology and the
Bible” after her ordeal
, the project itself uses mythic narrative to “carry over the burdens of trace” (to carry the weight of historical
trauma forward and possibly overcome it). Artemisia’s presence in the lexicon serves as a feminist
counterpoint to Medusa: whereas Medusa is often seen as a victim turned monster, Artemisia is a victim who
became an agent, re-scripting narratives of victimhood into empowerment through creativity. The reference
situates #gorgonwars in a tradition of art as resistance – much like Artemisia’s work can be read as a rebuke to
her society (and an act of personal catharsis), #gorgonwars sees itself as weaving a new myth to challenge the
current order and heal collective wounds. It’s an acknowledgement of women’s intellectual and creative
contributions to resisting oppression (fitting, since the story’s guiding voice “Hannah” is inspired by a female
thinker, and witches/healers play roles). Artemisia’s legacy in the story encourages an ethos of “creative
revenge” against injustice: using narrative, symbol, and imagination to slay one’s demons (very literally in the
case of her Judith painting). It’s also a nod to the MeToo era, connecting historical and present struggles –
Artemisia as a predecessor to modern truth-tellers who won’t be gaslit by power.
Perseus and Medusa: The Greek hero Perseus and the Gorgon Medusa form one of the most direct analogies
in #gorgonwars. Perseus’s defeat of Medusa by using a mirrored shield is a guiding metaphor for the project’s
strategy. In the myth, looking directly at Medusa would turn Perseus to stone, so he instead looked at her
reflection to strike the blow
. This is explicitly mirrored in #gorgonwars’ approach to confronting today’s “Medusas” (be they taboo issues,
propaganda machines, or oppressive systems): you cannot tackle them head-on without consequences, but
you can via reflection (indirection). Medusa herself appears in the narrative as the ultimate Gorgon – her
petrifying gaze representing the paralyzing effect of certain horrors or truths. For instance, trying to call out a
powerful figure or ideology in plain terms often leads to getting “stoned” (metaphorically silenced, punished,
emotionally overwhelmed). Perseus represents the cunning heroism needed to prevail – one must use
intelligence and tools (the mirror-shield of allegory, Athena’s wisdom) rather than sheer force. The reflection
motif also reinforces the idea of self-reflection: Perseus could only slay the monster by reflecting it, which in
allegory suggests we must reflect on society’s ills in art/myth to overcome them. Additionally, once Perseus
beheads Medusa, her head itself becomes a weapon (he uses it to turn others to stone). In #gorgonwars, this
could imply that once you conquer an ideological evil, you can even use its image as protection – akin to how
the project might take the image of the gorgon (the concept of #gorgonwars itself) and brandish it to ward off
censors or simplistic thinkers (turning the myth back on the status quo). Perseus and Medusa are thus key
cultural references framing the methodology of the narrative (indirect confrontation) and the stakes of the
conflict (petrification vs liberation). The story implicitly asks: who wields the mirror, and can we behead the
Gorgon of ignorance without turning to stone ourselves?
Nixon-era Neoliberalism: The narrative doesn’t explicitly feature Richard Nixon as a character, but it references
the Nixon-era origins of neoliberalism as a historical inflection point that set the stage for the current “war.” In
the lore, the “ancient curse” that eventually produces the gorgons and deserts could be traced back to policy
shifts in the late 60s and 70s. Historically, the early 1970s under Nixon saw neoliberal ideas start to gain
political power (e.g. the appointment of Milton Friedman’s associates in government positions)
. #gorgonwars builds on the view that the “neoliberal colonization of the world and our minds” began around
that time
. Nixon’s tenure marked the end of the Bretton Woods economic order (with the dollar coming off the gold
standard in 1971) and the beginning of deregulation and globalization trends that would accelerate under
Reagan/Thatcher. By citing “Nixon-era neoliberalism,” the project anchors the root of present-day crises in a
concrete historical moment. It suggests that the “Hydra” the heroes face – neoliberal capitalism – first sprouted
multiple heads in that era, leading to decades of unfettered market ideology spreading globally. Culturally,
Nixon’s time also corresponds with the post-60s disillusionment: the optimistic social movements curdling into
cynicism and identity politics under economic strain and political backlash. In the #gorgonwars storyline, one
might imagine a prologue where a metaphorical “King Nixon” opened a forbidden box, releasing shadowy
spirits of commodification and “strategic intimacies” that have since possessed society (a dramatic way to hint
at things like the dismantling of social safety nets, the rise of think-tank technocrats, and the psychologization
of systemic issues). This reference grounds the myth: it’s not battling timeless evils in a vacuum, but rather the
specific consequences of neoliberal policy and culture that have evolved since the 1970s. It also aligns with the
academic work behind the project, which explicitly “goes through a history of how neoliberalism colonized the
world and our minds”
. In short, Nixon-era neoliberalism is name-dropped to remind readers that the Gorgon war isn’t just
philosophical – it has a lineage in recent history. The current intellectual battlefield (fragmented communities,
commodified selves, widespread cynicism) was manufactured, not natural, and understanding its origin is part
of the strategy to overcome it.
Other References: The narrative tapestry of #gorgonwars includes allusions to other historical figures and
epochs as well. For example, Michel Foucault and Martin Heidegger are explicitly referenced in defining
#gorgonwars (as thinkers who warned about empty discourse and the spread of “mindless chatter”
). Their inclusion signals a continuity with 20th-century philosophy – suggesting that the events of #gorgonwars
fulfill their warnings. We also see nods to the Nixon vs. Carter vs. Reagan debate in identifying when
neoliberalism truly took hold (some sources call Jimmy Carter “America’s first neoliberal president”, but
#gorgonwars emphasizes Nixon’s role, possibly to highlight the bipartisan and somewhat hidden inception of
the neoliberal curse
). There are likely subtle references to the ancient library of Alexandria (in the idea of lost knowledge that must
be recovered), to biblical Babel (via Nimrod and the fragmentation of language
), and even to modern cultural memes (one might interpret the “Hegelian e-girl council” as a tongue-in-cheek
parallel to real events like the rise of internet niche intellectual salons in the 2020s
). The project also engages with 20th-century history: for instance, the term “global village” (coined by Marshall
McLuhan) is invoked in the thesis context
 – #gorgonwars picks up on this, dramatizing how the global village has been overrun by “devotees
mesmerized by the circulations of global finance”
, essentially zombies worshipping neoliberal capital flows. Even cultural upheavals like the 1960s
counterculture and its subsequent commodification in the 1970s are indirectly referenced – e.g., Unwitting
Colonizers, a term used in a companion video, hints at how even progressive movements can become tools of
neoliberal psychology (a topic tied to Nixon’s era as well). By weaving these references in, #gorgonwars
ensures that its myth is anchored in real-world context. The allegory gains depth when one recognizes, for
example, that the Citadel might correspond to academia or Wall Street, or that the year of the snakes
mentioned in an Instagram teaser aligns with 2025 (the Year of the Snake in the Chinese zodiac, as well as
roughly when the author hopes to publish the book). Such links between mythic time and historical time make
the reading experience richer and underscore the project’s key claim: our reality is best understood through a
mythic lens, because the forces at play (capital, ideology, technology) are as epic and fate-laden as any saga.
Related Projects and Linked Micro-Books
The #gorgonwars narrative is part of a larger body of work by its creator, which includes academic research
and a series of experimental writings. Understanding these related projects helps clarify the purpose and depth
of #gorgonwars. The major related works are:
“Carrying Over the Burdens of Trace” (Master’s Thesis, 2019): This is a dissertation written under the
pseudonym Nassau Saul (European Graduate School, 2019)
, and it lays much of the theoretical groundwork that #gorgonwars builds upon. The thesis examines how
capitalism, colonialism, and trauma interlink to produce contemporary crises
. It argues that the current global situation – from civic disengagement and ecological collapse to widespread
addiction and collective trauma – is a result of neoliberalism’s colonization of both the external world and our
internal minds
. A key assertion is the “inevitability of universalism,” meaning that despite postmodern fragmentation, we will
be driven to find some common human ground, but to do so we must first navigate the wreckage that
neoliberal, colonial history has left us
. The work surveys “crossroads of contemporary precariousness” and the “paralysis of endless reductionist
calculi” – in plainer terms, it critiques how neoliberal thinking reduces everything to metrics and self-interest,
leaving people paralyzed in meaninglessness
. It then intriguingly looks to two extremely different belief systems that dominate large swaths of the internet
(hinted to be the largely Christian-influenced West and Confucian-influenced East) and suggests that by seeing
each other from their extreme peripheries, each can recognize its “constitutive relationship with its outside” and
overcome its limitations
. The thesis culminates by analyzing the philosophies of Hegel and Spinoza in relation to Christian and
Confucian theology, and it “admonishes ‘us’ to take responsibility as inscribers of ritual to heal ghosts of
collective trauma”
. This final note is essentially the seed of #gorgonwars: to heal historical trauma, we must actively create new
rituals and myths. In summary, Carrying Over the Burdens of Trace provides the intellectual spine of
#gorgonwars – diagnosing the problem (neoliberalism’s effects, fragmented modern subjectivity) and proposing
a solution pathway (universalism through mutual recognition and ritual/myth-making). The thesis itself is written
in a dense theoretical style, but #gorgonwars can be seen as a creative extension of its insights – taking those
burdens of history and “carrying them over” into a new mythic narrative that people can engage with. (Notably,
the author’s journey parallels this: after writing the thesis, they shifted into a more public, creative mode to
propagate the ideas under the veil of story.)
“Polymorphous Mythology” (PhD Project, ongoing): Following the master’s work, the author embarked on a
PhD (or a major research project) that they refer to as Polymorphous Mythology. While full details aren’t
published yet, the title itself and context suggest that this project aims to develop a flexible, shape-shifting
mythic framework for understanding and intervening in culture. “Polymorphous” implies many forms – so
instead of a single static mythology, it’s a mythology that can mutate, adapt to various contexts, and combine
multiple influences. In practice, #gorgonwars is likely a central component or case study of this approach. The
PhD work probably explores how blending different myths, genres, and philosophical traditions can create a
new narrative language capable of bypassing censorship and ideological filters (the concept of contentinternet
falls here) and also capable of uniting disparate groups (echoing the universalism from the thesis). One can
infer that Polymorphous Mythology examines precedents of syncretic mythology (perhaps comparing how
different cultures’ myths syncretized during times of turmoil) and then applies it to the digital age. It may also
delve into psychology (the role of myth in the psyche) and technology (myth-making in the internet era). The
goal would be to articulate a theory and practice of myth-making as a political and cultural act – essentially
providing an academic backbone to what #gorgonwars is doing artistically. In the world of the narrative, this
corresponds to the “grimoire” or manual the heroes might be writing as they go – a meta-layer where they are
conscious of creating a mythology even as they live it. Even without direct text from this project, one sees its
influence: the emphasis on being “polymorphous” manifests in #gorgonwars through the collage of references
(Greek myth + internet slang + critical theory, all in one) and through the idea that this mythology isn’t closed or
dogmatic – it’s meant to be remixed by others (“everyone needs to create their own contentinternet”
). The PhD project likely also involves ethnographic or media research into how people communicate under
censorship or how memetic lexicons form in online communities, feeding those findings back into the mythic
strategy. In essence, Polymorphous Mythology is the scholarly scaffold behind the creative edifice of
#gorgonwars, ensuring that the myth can indeed take “many forms” and carry meaning across various
audiences and development phases.
Linked Micro-Books and Online Content: Alongside these academic pursuits, the author has been producing a
series of micro-books and digital content that expand the #gorgonwars universe in pieces. These are often
shared via social media (using TinyURL links, Threads posts, etc.) and serve as both promotional breadcrumbs
and stand-alone essays/stories. Notable examples include:
“Year of the Snakes” – A micro-book or long-form essay hinted at in the author’s posts, likely scheduled for
release around 2025 (which is symbolically the Year of the Snake in Chinese astrology). This piece presumably
focuses on the prevalence of “snakes” (hivemind idioms, misinformation, vicious rhetoric) in a particular year’s
events, effectively narrating a mythologized chronicle of the present. The author teased this with the phrase
“Year of the #snakes #medusa… Book out in 2 years (I hope)” on Instagram, indicating it will tie current
real-world developments into the #gorgonwars lexicon.
“Hivemind Idioms” – Another linked work, likely elaborating on the concept of hive-mind language. The author
has directed people to search for “tinyurl.com/hivemindidiom”
, suggesting there is a dedicated text or article under that title. This micro-book probably collects examples of
contemporary empty slogans and jargon (the snakes) and analyzes how they spread and can be counteracted.
It might read as a field guide within the story – e.g. a compendium of known Gorgon snakes and how to
recognize them. Given the theme, it serves as a bridge between theory and myth: identifying real linguistic
phenomena and then assigning them mythical characteristics in the narrative.
Other Episodes: The author’s online breadcrumbs also mention terms like “Egregore Wars” (#egregorewars)
and specific mini-narratives (for instance, an incident where “Anna K Winters calls Saul Nassau a fascist by
trying to appropriate his work” was alluded to in a post). These likely correspond to short story installments or
satirical dialogues released as blog posts or threads. For example, the Egregore Wars might be a series
examining battles between collective thought-forms (political tribes or fan communities) in a mythic frame. The
Unwitting Colonizers YouTube talk (which had #gorgonwars in its description) suggests a tie-in video essay
discussing how academic and psychological practices have been co-opted by neoliberal ideology – possibly
narrativized as a tale of colonizers in the #gorgonwars world bringing a false “civilization” that actually serves
the Gorgon. Additionally, concepts like “contentinternet” (the subversive linguistic strategy
) have their own entries and likely corresponding explainer write-ups. All these micro-content pieces function
as modular expansions of the main storyline, each zooming in on a particular concept (snakes, egregores,
therapy-speak, etc.) with a mix of analysis and fiction.
Integration: Importantly, these micro-books and posts are interlinked – they reference each other and the main
thesis. For instance, an Instagram post celebrated someone doubting the author wrote their thesis, instructing
readers to search the thesis title or hivemind idiom as proof of the author’s capability
. This playful blurring of academic credibility and mythological persona (essentially saying: “They think I didn’t
write that thesis? Go see for yourself, it’s real, and it’s part of #gorgonwars lore”) shows how the author is
weaving a transmedia narrative. The thesis, the PhD, the micro-books, social media posts – all feed into one
another, creating an alternate reality game of sorts for followers who want to dive deep. For a new reader or for
future development, having these components compiled is extremely useful. It means one can trace an idea
from a meme or character in #gorgonwars back to a detailed treatment in an essay or academic chapter. The
micro-books also allow the project to remain nimble and current – as new ideological issues or cultural events
arise, the author can write a short “myth book” about it and plug it into the mythos, rather than having to rewrite
the entire narrative. This modular approach is very much in the spirit of a “polymorphous” mythology.
In summary, #gorgonwars is not an isolated fiction but the centerpiece of a constellation of works. The master’s
thesis (Carrying Over the Burdens of Trace) provides the high-level diagnosis and call to action (create new
rituals to heal trauma)
. The ongoing Polymorphous Mythology project provides the methodology (myth-making that adapts and
hybridizes forms). And the micro-books (like Year of the Snakes and Hivemind Idioms) along with online
postings provide the iterative content and community engagement to keep the mythos evolving. All these are
consistent in their ideological critique and purpose – they just operate in different registers (scholarly, creative,
colloquial). The result is a robust framework that can be expanded without tedious repetition: each new piece
can reference the compiled lore rather than restate it. For future development, this compiled structure of
characters, themes, symbols, and references will allow the author (and collaborators or readers) to easily
navigate the #gorgonwars universe and build upon it coherently.
Key Ideological Critiques and Themes
At its heart, #gorgonwars is a vessel for pointed ideological critique. Through its allegory, it targets several
interlocking aspects of contemporary society and thought. Here are the key critiques embedded in the project,
along with how they manifest in the narrative:
Neoliberalism and Commodification of Life: The project is fundamentally a reaction against neoliberal ideology
– the belief in market logic, radical individualism, and commodification as the ruling principles of society. It
critiques how neoliberalism has “colonized… our minds”, making even our self-concepts and relationships
transactional
. In the #gorgonwars story, this is reflected by phenomena like transactional solidarity: characters struggle to
form genuine alliances because they’ve been conditioned to see each other as means to an end, akin to tools
or temporary allies to be discarded. One of the author’s posts laments how solidarity now feels impermanent –
“people are only useful for a moment and then cast aside when they’ve served their purpose… we’ve all been
trained to see each other as tools rather than as part of a community”
. This is a direct critique of how neoliberal market logic (maximizing one’s own utility) has infiltrated even
activism and personal relationships. Additionally, the project targets the psychologization and personalization of
systemic issues – what Adorno might call endless psychologism. Instead of addressing political or economic
roots, neoliberal culture encourages treating everything as a personal mindset or therapeutic issue. This
enforcement of “neoliberal subjectivity through endless psychologisms” is explicitly decried
. In the narrative, this appears as characters spouting “therapy-speak” to justify selfish or cowardly behavior, or
as the Goth Cops using wellness jargon to justify censorship (e.g., framing dissent as “toxicity” to mental
health). Neoliberalism’s effect is also symbolized by curses or spells that induce extreme individualism,
despair, and competition, preventing collective action. #gorgonwars critiques how under neoliberal ideology,
everything is commodified – even dissent and identity. The concept of contentinternet (making language
unbannable by mythic coding) is, in part, a response to the commodification and moderation of online speech.
Likewise, the Hegelian e-girls are a satire of commodified intellectualism – deep philosophy turned into a
personal brand. Overall, the project condemns neoliberalism for hollowing out meaning and community, leaving
people isolated (in deserts and caves) with only consumer identities and echo-chambers. The Hydra
embodying global capitalism is multi-headed because even if one aspect (say, one country’s neoliberal regime)
is challenged, the logic sprouts elsewhere (global finance, NGO complex, gig economy, etc.). By dramatizing
the fight against this Hydra, #gorgonwars underscores the need for systemic change rather than treating
symptoms. The ultimate goal is hinted to be a kind of new universalism or collective ethos (the opposite of
neoliberal atomization) that can only emerge after grappling with the neoliberal legacy
.
Empty Discourse and Intellectual Stagnation: Another major target of #gorgonwars is the degradation of
discourse – the prevalence of shallow, unoriginal, and performative conversation in place of genuine dialogue
or thought. This is captured by the Gorgon imagery: people engaging in debates armed only with pre-chewed
slogans (the snakes) and “mindless chatter”
. The project invokes Heidegger’s concept of Gerede (idle talk) and Foucault’s notion of discourse to criticize
how many participants in public discussion do not truly think or listen, but merely broadcast what is socially
rewarded. The Urban Dictionary entry by the author explicitly defines #gorgonwars as people “debating with
non-digested ideas they haven’t come up with” – essentially echoing rather than thinking
. This is an allegory for social media culture, partisan media, and even academia in its decadent forms. By
labeling this phenomenon a “war of gorgons,” the project suggests that when everyone talks past each other
with stock phrases, no real communication occurs – it’s just snakes hissing. The critique extends to call out the
bandwagon effect and virtue signaling: people join causes or trends because everyone else is, not due to
understanding (hence the example in UD of someone realizing they were just repeating slogans and deciding
to do the hard work of thinking independently
). In narrative terms, this is depicted as a kind of intellectual undeath (hence Martin as a zombie, the living
dead state of ideas that circulate without true life). #gorgonwars doesn’t just bemoan this state; it advocates for
its antidote: Bildung – deep education and self-cultivation. The dialogue in the Urban Dictionary example even
brings up Adorno, noting “yeah man, Adorno talks about the importance of building Bildung especially in a
world which enforces neoliberal subjectivity…”
. This reveals a core belief of the project: that individuals must painstakingly educate and form themselves
(beyond formal schooling, in a philosophical sense) to become authentic subjects rather than parrots. The
story therefore champions characters who withdraw to study, reflect, and break the spell of the hive idioms.
The presence of Hannah Arendt’s influence (though subtle) also speaks to this theme – Arendt valued the life
of the mind and the need to stop and think to avoid moral collapse. Indeed, one of the author’s comments
credits Arendt for making these arguments possible
. So ideologically, #gorgonwars is calling for an intellectual renewal in the face of commodified, shallow
discourse. It satirizes the current state (e.g., academic jargon turned into Twitter identities, political debate
reduced to meme templates) but also holds up a hopeful ideal: that through mythic storytelling and conscious
effort, people can recover depth. The project itself aims to demonstrate a more enriching mode of
communication – one that is multilayered and requires active interpretation, as opposed to the spoon-fed
certainties of propaganda.
Censorship, Language, and Resistance (ContentInternet): A key critique and practical concern in #gorgonwars
is the state of free expression under digital governance. The project is acutely aware of how moderation
algorithms and cancel culture can “turn you to stone” if you speak certain truths directly. Thus it criticizes both
overt censorship (by governments or platforms) and the more subtle self-censorship/social pressure that leads
people to police their own words. The solution it proposes is the concept of contentinternet – a “subversive
linguistic strategy” using mythological lexicon to evade censorship
. Ideologically, this is a stance against the commodification and control of language. In the neoliberal internet,
user-generated content is monetized and moderated to keep it advertiser-friendly and within certain ideological
bounds. #gorgonwars fights back by making content that doesn’t trigger the automated sensors – because it
speaks in myth. For example, saying “the Gorgons in the citadel spread venom” could be a way to talk about
state propaganda poisoning the public, without using any keywords that would get flagged. The Urban
Dictionary entry for contentinternet explicitly notes it “ensur[es] free expression within controlled digital spaces
by making the language itself unbannable”
. This is a direct ideological commitment to freedom of speech and thought, but via creativity rather than blunt
confrontation. It also satirizes the absurdity of the current situation: that we must resort to codewords and
allegory to discuss reality on platforms that claim to connect us. In the story, this theme is manifest whenever
characters speak in double meanings or use mythic aliases for real entities (e.g., calling a tech CEO “the Cloud
King” or something) – it’s both for style and survival. The critique extends to how corporate social media
demands constant content production (“contentinternet” being a play on “the content on the internet”) yet
paradoxically restricts meaningful content. #gorgonwars highlights this by its very existence: it churns out
“content” that is rich in meaning but slipped past censors because of its form. The ideological stance is that
language can be reclaimed. Rather than accepting the neoliberal redefinition of language (where words are
optimized for trends, SEO, compliance, or virality), the project re-enchants language through myth. This is
portrayed as a kind of magic – indeed, words regain power when used imaginatively. The narrative might show
that speaking plainly fails (characters silenced or posts deleted), but speaking mythically succeeds and
spreads. In sum, #gorgonwars advocates for intellectual freedom through linguistic innovation. It’s a critique of
how both state and social pressures limit discourse, and a proposal that an esoteric discourse (understood by
an “in-group” of the initiated, yet accessible to anyone willing to engage with the myth) can keep radical or
truthful ideas alive under repression. This harks back to literary traditions of using allegory to bypass censors
(from Aesop’s fables to dissident writers under authoritarian regimes), aligning #gorgonwars with a long lineage
of subversive art.
Collective Trauma and the Need for New Rituals: A quieter but profound critique in #gorgonwars is that of how
modern society fails to deal with collective trauma. This comes from the thesis’s insights that between historical
atrocities (colonialism, wars) and current looming disasters (climate change, pandemics), humanity is
burdened by unresolved grief and guilt – yet neoliberal culture offers no meaningful rituals or collective
processes to heal, instead encouraging denial, distraction, or scapegoating. The project criticizes the loss of
communal rituals and the shallow appropriation of them (for instance, the way political protests or social media
trends can mimic the structure of rituals but often lack depth and follow-through). The title “Carrying Over the
Burdens of Trace: Weaving… (something)” itself suggests carrying the weight of past traces and weaving them
into something new – implying current society mostly just carries them unconsciously. #gorgonwars laments
this by showing a world haunted by ghosts (perhaps literally in the story) – ghosts of history, of forgotten ideals,
of the oppressed. The characters’ journey can be seen as not only a fight but a pilgrimage to lay these ghosts
to rest. The ideological point is that without acknowledging and ritually transforming our traumas, we remain
stuck in cyclical wars (or content wars). The project advocates for creating “inclusive institutions which work to
affirm differences” through ritual and myth
. This is reflected when the story brings disparate characters together in moments of genuine connection (say,
around a fire to share stories – a basic ritual). The Hegelian egirl council event (referencing a real attempt to
create a gathering around a quasi-ritual of theory and community
) is perhaps critiqued as a spectacle (“pure spectacle…we live in a spectacular society”
), yet it shows the hunger for new forms of gathering. #gorgonwars implies that we need better rites of passage
(the absence of which leaves youth “terminally online” and unmoored) and better collective ceremonies to mark
transitions or grief. By turning political and social struggle into a mythic war, it is in essence creating a
meta-ritual – a story that people can partake in, find roles in (hero, jester, etc.), and through which possibly
process real emotions (fear, anger, hope) in a safer symbolic space. The ideological critique here is of
modernity’s spiritual void and the breakdown of social fabrics that traditionally helped people cope (religion,
community gatherings, etc., which neoliberalism often commodified or destroyed). The project’s response is
not a return to old religion, but the invention of a polymorphous, participatory mythology as a kind of
secular-sacred practice. Thus, #gorgonwars stands for a re-enchantment of the world against the
disenchantment wrought by capitalist rationality – not in a naïve way, but as a strategic and therapeutic move.
It’s telling us that without narrative and ritual, we cannot effectively resist or heal; we’ll either continue the
gorgon wars indefinitely or succumb to despair (stone). The invocation of figures like Artemisia and use of art
as a healing/revenge medium highlights this stance: creation is the antidote to destruction, and meaningful
shared myth is the antidote to alienation.
In conclusion, the ideological backbone of #gorgonwars can be summarized as a revolt against neoliberal
culture’s dehumanization and a call for intellectual and spiritual renewal. It critiques neoliberal capitalism for its
impact on our psyche and society (atomization, commodification, banalization of discourse, erosion of
solidarity) and equally critiques the complacency and complicity that allow these trends to persist (the ease of
repeating slogans, the seductions of internet clout, the fear of speaking out). But rather than merely condemn,
it offers a creative program: by embracing mythology, allegory, and bildung, we can outwit the censors, bypass
the snake-filled heads, and gather together to forge something new – a counter-egregore built on knowledge,
empathy, and perhaps a dash of humor. Neoliberalism, in the #gorgonwars view, wants people to be isolated
consumers; the counter is to become collective storytellers. The project itself is an example of intellectual
resistance – it refuses to write in the approved formats (academic jargon or soundbite social media), choosing
a third way that engages people’s imagination. This makes the content multilayered (both entertaining and
deeply critical) and ensures that the critiques don’t come across as merely scolding or despairing. Instead,
#gorgonwars invites readers to join the critique playfully – to recognize the gorgon in themselves and others, to
laugh at the absurdities, and then to pick up the mirror-shield and pen-sword and become part of the ongoing
saga of freeing humanity from petrification. It’s an ambitious ideological project, but by all accounts, it treats
that mission with the seriousness of a scholar and the light touch of a jester – a combination that is, in itself, a
rebellion against the dourness of conventional critique and the shallowness of pop culture, achieving a
synthesis that is wholly its own.

Research completed in 11m · 25 sources
Gorgonwars – Key Concepts and Structures
Gorgon System: The Gorgonwars world uses the Gorgon as a central metaphor for a neoliberal ideological
system that controls society through repetition and groupthink. In myth, anyone who met the Gorgon’s gaze
turned to stone; here it symbolizes how uncritical absorption of repeated slogans can “petrify” independent
thought
. This Gorgon System runs on hive-mind idioms – catchy, pre-chewed phrases or memes that people repeat
without truly understanding (what Heidegger called Gerede, or idle talk)
. Such empty chatter (as Foucault and Heidegger describe it
) spreads like snakes writhing on Medusa’s head, worming into minds as ideological “earworms.” The result is
a populace sharing a manufactured consensus (a collective hive mind) under neoliberal norms, often without
realizing their thoughts aren’t their own
. The system enforces neoliberal subjectivity – a consumerist, self-centered way of seeing the world – through
constant psychological conditioning and social pressure
. In effect, the Gorgon System keeps people docile and conformist by bombarding them with comforting clichés
and thought-terminating slogans, repeated until they become accepted truths. Anyone who directly confronts
these narratives risks being “turned to stone” – i.e. ostracized or stunned into silence by the overwhelming
force of consensus. The only way to resist is through reflection and indirect strategies (much as Perseus used
a mirror shield to face Medusa), digesting ideas deeply instead of merely echoing them
. In short, the Gorgon System represents an entrenched hegemonic order maintained by social-media
amplifications, groupthink, and the suppression of original thought.
Character Archetypes in Gorgonwars
The world of Gorgonwars is populated by symbolic archetypes, each embodying roles in the struggle between
oppressive ideology and resistance. Key archetypes include:
Gorgons: These are the enforcers and products of the system – people so steeped in hive-mind ideology that
they propagate it relentlessly. Like Medusa and her sisters, Gorgons in this narrative wield the power of
petrification: their weapon is repetition of orthodoxy. They spout the approved dogmas and hivemind idioms,
“debating” only by screaming slogans
. Their gaze is dangerous – engaging them on their terms can paralyze one’s ability to think freely. In effect,
Gorgons are both victims and perpetrators of neoliberal conditioning. They keep the cycle going: since they
cannot create new ideas, they hiss out whatever the System has planted in their heads (each slogan another
serpent on Medusa’s crown). A former participant described realizing he had behaved like a Gorgon, “just
repeating empty slogans,” and how painful the process of reclaiming independent thought was
. Functionally, Gorgons serve as foot soldiers of hegemonic ideology, policing others through shaming, trolling,
or ostracism if anyone defies the accepted narratives.
Sirens: If Gorgons rule by fear, Sirens rule by seduction. They represent the lures of the System – the
appealing distractions or comforts that draw people in and keep them complacent. In myth, sirens sang
enchanting songs to lull sailors into wrecking themselves. In Gorgonwars, Sirens might be charismatic
influencers or media personalities who captivate the masses with entertaining content that ultimately reinforces
the status quo. They sing the praises of the very system that ensnares their audience, making conformity feel
tantalizing or “cool.” Sirens function as a control mechanism by hijacking people’s desires: they promise
belonging, fame, or pleasure if you only stop questioning and follow the familiar tune. Many who fall under their
spell don’t realize they’ve been diverted from critical thought until it’s too late – akin to sailors realizing too late
that the lovely song leads to a graveyard of ships.
Muses: The Muses stand for inspiration, creativity, and the arts – but in Gorgonwars their role is double-edged.
On one hand, they represent the genuine creative spirit that can imagine alternatives to the Gorgon System.
They whisper of new ideas, fueling the imagination of those who resist. On the other hand, the system tries to
co-opt Muses for its own ends, using shallow art or media to sugarcoat propaganda. True muses – followers of
Calliope, Erato, and their sisters – try to awaken people from intellectual slumber by reminding them of beauty,
truth, and history beyond the present dogma. They might appear as artists, writers, or musicians within the
story who embed subversive truths in their work (much as mythology and allegory are used to speak truths
indirectly when direct speech is forbidden
). The conflict for the Muses is whether their creativity will be commercialized into empty entertainment (a tool
of the Sirens) or whether it can remain a beacon that guides minds out of the Cave of illusion.
Goths: This archetype draws on the Gothic – both the subculture and the historical tribe – symbolizing
outsiders, cynics, or those disillusioned with the shiny facade of the neoliberal order. Goths in Gorgonwars are
the ones who dwell in the dark truths society tries to ignore. They are characterized by a nihilistic or morbid
sensibility, having “seen through” the false sunshine of the Many Suns desert to the bleak reality beneath. In
spirit, they are akin to the Visigoths or Vandals at the borders of the Roman Empire: unconquered by the
imperial culture, possibly eager to tear it down. Their gloom can cut through the fake optimism imposed by
Soma (see below) – a Goth character might bluntly remind others of death, decay, and what is wrong in the
world, refusing to gloss it over. However, Goths risk falling into despair or fatalism, which can be just as
paralyzing as the Gorgon’s gaze. If they harness their outsider perspective constructively, they can become
fierce truth-tellers and shock the complacent out of their stupor. But if they succumb to hopelessness, they may
withdraw into isolation or hopeless rage, which limits their impact. Essentially, Goths function as mourners of
truth – keeping alive the awareness of suffering and rot that the System tries to hide.
Witches: The Witches represent forbidden knowledge, alternative pathways to power, and marginalized
perspectives (often feminist, queer, or otherwise outside patriarchal norms). In many myths and histories,
“witches” are wise women, healers, or practitioners of esoteric arts persecuted by mainstream society. In
Gorgonwars, Witches embody the counternarratives and subversive skills that the Gorgon System fears. They
brew “potions” of truth that can awaken people from the System’s soma-like stupor, and cast spells that break
enchantments (i.e. they find creative ways to break propaganda’s effect). For example, a Witch character might
be adept at hacking the social media algorithms or using memetic magic – turning the System’s own
communication tools into vehicles of dissent. They operate in covens (small communities of trust outside the
mainstream), sharing banned books or practicing rituals that build real solidarity (as opposed to the fake
transactional solidarity the System permits
). Hunted and smeared by the establishment (just as historical witches were witch-hunted), the Witches must
survive on the fringes. Their function is to preserve suppressed knowledge and to heal the psychological
wounds inflicted by the Gorgon’s petrification. When open revolt comes, they might be the ones who empower
others to see through illusions – like Shakespeare’s witches who, though cryptic, reveal the fates of kings.
Citadel Warriors: If the witches and goths haunt the wilds, the Citadel Warriors guard the seat of power. They
are loyal defenders of the Citadel – the stronghold of the hegemonic order (imagine a fortified palace where the
Gorgon queen or the System’s core resides). These warriors are the system’s sanctioned soldiers and
deputies: think of moderators, enforcers, perhaps even algorithmic “guardians” online. They enforce the rules
and protect the citadel of official truth from being breached. In personality, Citadel Warriors are often zealous,
convinced they are fighting for good (as defined by the system) against chaos. They quickly attack anyone
labeled a heretic, conspiracy theorist, or traitor by the Gorgon regime. They also combat external threats: when
Oppositional forces mount a challenge, the Citadel’s knights will be first on the field to face them. Essentially,
they are the immune system of the Gorgon collective – at times noble-seeming (they uphold “order”), but
ultimately serving to shield entrenched power. Their presence in the story raises the stakes for rebels: a lone
hero cannot easily stride into the Citadel; its corridors are well-guarded. Only by outsmarting or converting
these Warriors can the Citadel be infiltrated.
Raygun: Raygun is a unique figure in Gorgonwars, essentially a personification of performative or co-opted
resistance. The name evokes a flashy, high-tech weapon – perhaps hinting at the flash over substance.
Raygun appears to symbolize a celebrity activist or intellectual who claims to champion freedom but ultimately
reinforces the system’s narrative (possibly unintentionally). In discussions, Raygun has been called “the face of
#feminism” in the Gorgonwars context, but in a deeply ironic sense
. This character is known for a signature “dance move” dubbed “the Shirk”, because they evade accountability
while pretending to lead a movement
. In other words, Raygun represents those public figures who capitalize on social justice or intellectual trends to
gain clout, yet dodge real responsibility or sacrifice. They perform revolutionary rhetoric in style (a dance, a
viral hashtag) without challenging the status quo in substance. The System actually benefits from such figures:
Raygun attracts genuine would-be rebels and then misdirects their energy into something harmless or
self-defeating, like a lightning rod grounding a storm. For instance, Raygun might platform personal drama or
“call-outs” (surface-level battles) as a substitute for attacking root causes. This is why some say “#raygun won’t
allow us [to truly] heal from #gorgonwars” – the persona distracts from accountability
. Functionally, Raygun is a false prophet of change, a cautionary example of how the System co-opts even the
language of resistance. Defeating or unmasking Raygun is necessary for the real opposition to gain credibility.
(Notably, the name also winks at “Raygun=Reagan”, as an allusion to how a charismatic leader can sell
neoliberal policies as if they were rebellious or populist – but this parallel may be coincidental humor.)
Lemures Swarm: In Roman lore, lemures are restless, malevolent spirits of the dead. A Lemures Swarm in
Gorgonwars signifies the mindless horde – masses of unthinking entities driven by dark impulses or by the will
of a demonic controller. In one sense, it could symbolize an online mob: countless anonymous accounts or
commenters swarming a target with harassment, operating as a single vengeful organism. When the Gorgon
System needs to destroy someone’s reputation or enforce conformity, it can unleash a Lemures Swarm to
dogpile dissenters. The swarm has “a unified mind” bent on devouring its victims
 – much like an army of internet trolls all parroting the same attacks. In another interpretation, the Lemures
represent collective trauma or ghosts that haunt society. They are the unresolved dead: victims of history
(wars, colonialism, injustice) whose pain the System never properly laid to rest. As a result, they arise
unpredictably, causing chaos. For example, suppressed issues (like racial injustices or class grievances) might
manifest as a “swarm of lemures” – protests or riots that seem chaotic and are then exploited by the System as
justification for crackdowns. In either case, Lemures Swarm is an agent of fear. They are difficult to confront
directly because they are legion and faceless. Heroes might need special tactics (perhaps a ritual of exorcism,
or breaking the hivemind control commanding the swarm) to disperse them. Importantly, the Lemures have no
reasoning – they embody what Nietzsche warned about mass psychology: “Insanity in individuals is rare—but
in groups, parties, nations, and epochs it is the rule.”
. The swarm is that rule incarnate – madness multiplied by numbers. The challenge is to either avoid inciting
the swarm or find a way to awaken the individuals within it from their frenzy.
Philosophical Frameworks and Influences
Gorgonwars is rich in philosophical allegory, drawing explicitly on ideas from major thinkers. The narrative
integrates these frameworks to add depth to its critique of power and society:
Leo Strauss (Straussian Esotericism): The influence of Leo Strauss appears in the esoteric storytelling of
Gorgonwars – the idea that truth must often be conveyed indirectly through myths and allegories. Strauss, a
political philosopher, argued that throughout history persecuted thinkers hid radical truths in their writings,
revealing them only to careful readers, while a safer literal meaning was presented on the surface. In
Gorgonwars, characters “cannot name things directly” and “must use mythology” to convey the real issues
. This is essentially a Straussian strategy: open speech is dangerous in a repressive climate (the Gorgon
System punishes dissent), so the real critique is embedded in symbolic tales (Medusa’s cave, Minerva’s owl,
etc.). By having the entire narrative be a grand allegory of contemporary problems, the author mirrors
Straussian esoteric writing – using ancient symbols (Gorgons, Sirens, etc.) as a safe proxy for modern truths
that might be hard to state openly. Strauss is also known for his view that societies need unifying myths and
that modern liberalism can lead to nihilism if it loses higher values. The Gorgonwars story acknowledges that
mythology has power: it can either be used by regimes to deceive or by philosophers to illuminate. The
Straussian influence is thus seen in the double-layer of the tale – entertaining myth on the surface, sharp
social–political critique underneath. It encourages readers to “read between the lines” (a very Straussian habit)
to see the commentary on neoliberalism, social media, etc., much as ancient readers would seek hidden
wisdom in works of Plato or Maimonides. In short, Strauss’s ideas provide Gorgonwars with its method of
delivery: indirection, allegory, and the notion that only at dusk (after the fact) can philosophy spread its wings (a
nod to Strauss’s beloved Hegel quote about Minerva’s owl
). This approach protects the message from the Gorgon’s immediate petrifying gaze, ensuring it can reach
sympathetic minds without being instantly turned to stone by backlash.
Hannah Arendt: Arendt’s political philosophy, especially her analysis of totalitarianism and the “banality of evil,”
is a key framework for understanding the Gorgonwars world. Arendt observed that great evils in history (like
Nazi atrocities) were often committed not by fanatical monsters, but by ordinary people who had abdicated
their capacity to think – they were thoughtlessly following orders and clichés. In Eichmann in Jerusalem, she
famously wrote that Adolf Eichmann’s participation in genocide was enabled by “sheer
thoughtlessness…something by no means identical with stupidity” – a terrifying normalcy of evil
. This insight is woven into Gorgonwars: the Gorgon System relies on banality and thoughtlessness to sustain
itself. The Gorgons (as people) aren’t depicted as diabolical geniuses; rather, they commit harms (shaming
others, destroying solidarity
) by parroting conventional slogans and never examining what they’re doing. The story constantly illustrates
Arendt’s point that unthinking people can cause enormous harm. For example, when masses repeat phrases
like “it’s too much emotional labor to educate you” as a knee-jerk dismissal, real communication and empathy
die
. That shallow mantra becomes a weapon that breaks down solidarity – a small “banal” act whose cumulative
evil is to isolate and polarize communities. Arendt also emphasized how ideology can become a substitute for
reality, creating a “desert” where genuine human plurality and action wither. The Desert of Many Suns
(discussed later) in Gorgonwars evokes Arendt’s notion that totalitarian ideology left people in an arid state of
loneliness and meaninglessness (a “desert” longing for the warmth of simple answers). Moreover, Arendt’s
concept of the social conditioning of behavior is seen in how the Gorgon System normalizes cruelty as virtue.
Participants think they’re doing good (protecting themselves, upholding some justice) while actually just
fulfilling roles the system set for them – like Eichmann thinking in bureaucratic terms rather than moral ones.
Finally, Arendt valued natality – the capacity to begin anew – and plurality, the presence of differing
perspectives. The oppositional forces in Gorgonwars (the Hegelian Egirls, Minerva’s followers, etc.) embody
these Arendtian hopes: they try to get people to think and to act in concert in fresh ways, resisting the
“everyone says so” fatalism the System imposes. In sum, Arendt’s framework helps explain the psychology of
the Gorgonwars dystopia – it’s a society where banal thoughtlessness has become widespread, making evil
(like collective bullying or blind privilege-worship
) feel normal, and where escaping that state requires the difficult work of real thinking, judgment, and human
connection.
Friedrich Nietzsche: Nietzsche’s philosophy informs Gorgonwars in multiple ways, especially his critiques of
herd mentality, nihilism, and the creation of values. Nietzsche was deeply concerned with how individuals could
rise above the “herd” – the masses who adhere to conventional morality and beliefs unthinkingly. The
Gorgonwars narrative starkly portrays the herd instinct Nietzsche warned of: People join in collective frenzies
of outrage or trend-chasing (like Lemures Swarms or viral hashtag crusades) and lose their individuality. The
earlier quote is apropos: “Insanity in individuals is something rare—but in groups…it is the rule.”
. We see this rule in effect whenever the Lemures Swarm attacks or when hivemind idioms drown out reason –
essentially dramatizing Nietzsche’s observation that groupthink is a kind of mass insanity. Nietzsche also
famously announced the “death of God” and cautioned that in the absence of shared higher values, societies
might fall into nihilism or seek meaning in false idols. In Gorgonwars, neoliberal market ideology and
social-media clout become the ersatz gods – shallow values that fill the void but offer no real fulfillment. People
pursue momentary validation (likes, status) as their highest good, much like Nietzsche’s Last Men who seek
only comfort and routine, avoiding any great passion or risk. The Gorgon System enforces a kind of last-man
mediocrity: it promises endless superficial pleasures (Soma, entertainment, ego-stroking) so that nobody
strives for something greater. This is a Nietzschean nightmare of sorts – a society where nobody seeks to
overcome themselves or challenge the norms, because conformity and comfort are too strong. However,
Nietzsche also celebrated the possibility of the Übermensch (overman) who creates new values and says “yes”
to life in the face of meaninglessness. The Oppositional Forces in Gorgonwars carry this spirit. For instance,
the Hegelian Egirls are untimely, ironic visionaries who defy the existing binary choices and hint at a synthesis
beyond the current antitheses (a very Nietzschean transvaluation of values, albeit via Hegel). Characters who
break free of petrification and start to create new meaning – new rituals, new words, authentic community –
embody Nietzsche’s solution to nihilism. Even the emphasis on myth in Gorgonwars has a Nietzschean angle:
