Literature Review for the Gorgonwars Mythological-Philosophical Project
This literature review compiles highly cited or citation-rich academic works by lesser-known scholars that
critically engage with neoliberal subjectivity, narrative ideology, digital symbolism, social media structures,
philosophical dialectics, allegorical literature, comparative mythology, and polymorphous mythology. These
works challenge dominant discursive norms and avoid mere repetition of canonical theories, providing original
insights relevant to Gorgonwars. Below, the references are grouped by thematic focus, each with a brief
description and key citations.
Critical Analyses of Neoliberal Subjectivity and Ideology
Thomas Teo (2018), “Homo neoliberalus: From personality to forms of subjectivity” – A highly cited article in
Theory & Psychology that sketches the ideal-typical neoliberal self. Teo describes how under neoliberalism
individuals internalize market logic: “thinking about liberty dominates thinking about equality or solidarity” and
personal problems are tackled with individualized solutions rather than collective action
. This critical psychology perspective exposes the mindset of homo neoliberalus and has been influential in
discussions of how ideology shapes subjectivity.
Jodi Dean (2005), “Communicative Capitalism: Circulation and the Foreclosure of Politics” – A widely cited
work (1200+ citations) that critiques how democratic ideals have been subsumed by networked digital media.
Dean coins “communicative capitalism”, in which values like participation and free expression “take material
form in networked communications technologies”, yet “the deluge of screens and spectacles” ends up
undermining political equality and efficacy
. This analysis shows how social-media structures create an ideological feedback loop that depoliticizes
subjects, offering a fresh dialectical critique of neoliberal democracy.
Byung-Chul Han (2017), Psychopolitics: Neoliberalism and New Technologies of Power – A short book by
philosopher Han (often cited in critical theory circles) examining how neoliberal power operates through
internalized self-discipline. Han argues that in today’s data-driven society, individuals become self-exploiters,
voluntarily optimizing and branding themselves as if they were market products. Although not a canonical
figure, Han’s work challenges the “hivemind” of neoliberal positivity by using a dialectical lens to reveal the
psychic violence of constant productivity (e.g. the “achievement-subject” who blames themselves, not society,
for any failure). Psychopolitics is frequently referenced for its original insight into digital-age subjectivity (e.g.
linking the rise of burnout and depression to neoliberal selfhood).
Narrative, Myth, and Allegory as Ideology Critique
Bruce Lincoln (1999), Theorizing Myth: Narrative, Ideology, and Scholarship – An influential book by
comparative mythologist Bruce Lincoln that reconceptualizes myth as a form of ideology in narrative form
. Lincoln avoids romanticized or “hivemind” takes on myth; instead, he demonstrates through case studies that
myths (and even scholarship about myth) often serve political and social interests. By tracing mythmaking from
ancient Greece to modern nationalism, Lincoln’s deeply reflective analysis challenges dominant myth theories
(e.g. Eliade or Campbell) and provides a critical framework to read mythology as a tool of social power and
subject formation.
Sarker Hasan Al Zayed (2023), Allegories of Neoliberalism: Contemporary South Asian Fictions, Capital, and
Utopia – A recent scholarly monograph that merges literary criticism with political economy. Zayed explicitly
critiques “Foucauldian governmentalist” takes on neoliberalism, instead using a historical-materialist approach
to read South Asian literature as allegories of neoliberal capitalism
. Through novels by authors like Aravind Adiga, Arundhati Roy, and Mohsin Hamid, the book examines how
fiction portrays capitalism’s hidden structures – what Marx called its “forms of appearance”
. By allegorizing issues like commodification, class exploitation, and financialization in narrative form, these
works reveal the commitments and consequences of neoliberal ideology, often envisioning utopian alternatives.
Zayed’s analysis is citation-rich and illustrates how comparative mythology and allegorical literature can
illuminate real socio-economic conditions.
Geordie Miller (2020), “An Allegory of Value: American Literature within Neoliberalism” – A doctoral thesis
(Dalhousie University) that offers an allegorical reading of late-20th-century American fiction (authors like
William Gaddis, David Foster Wallace, George Saunders) as a critical response to neoliberal ideology. Miller
finds that these novels dramatize how neoliberal discourse replaces collective values with market logic. For
instance, in Saunders’s stories “the collective quality of the class struggle is delegitimized through the
individualizing effects of entrepreneurship and consumer culture”, demonstrating the power of allegory to
expose neoliberal assumptions
. The thesis concludes that literature serves as a medium to lay bare the often-unquestioned “commitments
and consequences” of neoliberal thought
. Although a dissertation, this work reflects deep analysis and is supported by extensive citations, aligning with
Gorgonwars’s ethos of challenging normative narratives.
Mark Fisher (2009), Capitalist Realism: Is There No Alternative? – A notable but non-canonical critique (widely
discussed in niche academic and art circles) which argues that late capitalist society has so pervaded
imagination that it’s easier to imagine the end of the world than the end of capitalism. Fisher’s concept of
“capitalist realism” complements the above works by explaining the narrative ideology that stifles dissent: mass
media and popular culture continually re-inscribe the message that there is no alternative. While written in an
accessible style (not a formal study), the book has proven highly influential (hundreds of scholarly citations) for
its original diagnosis of our era’s ideological “myth” – a pervasive sense of inevitability that Gorgonwars seeks
to symbolically break.
Digital Mythologies and Networked Structures
Denis S. Artamonov et al. (2021), “Digital Mythology: A New Direction in the Study of Social Myths” – A recent
academic article that examines how mythology is transformed by the digital turn
. The authors introduce “digital mythology” as a system of social myths born in virtual spaces, noting that any
internet user can become a myth-maker in online communities
. Digital myths are created when individuals blend mass culture images with personal narratives and
disseminate them via social media
. Critically, these myths then circulate as part of everyday reality – for example, “digital myths are embodied in
selfbranding, [post-truth] fakes, Internet memes, [and] computer games”, becoming “an integral part of the
digital world.”
. The study emphasizes that the flood of information in the digital age causes myth to “mutate into new forms”,
and even digital technology itself gets mythologized as a way to make sense of the world
. This citation-rich work (grounded in digital humanities) provides a framework for understanding polymorphous
myth-making on social platforms – directly relevant to Gorgonwars’ interest in digital symbolism and narrative
structures.
Paolo Bory (2020), The Internet Myth: From the Internet Imaginary to Network Ideologies – A scholarly book
(open access from University of Westminster Press) that retraces the ideological narratives behind the
Internet’s development. Bory challenges the “myth” that digital networks by themselves drive social progress,
showing instead how a dominant narrative was constructed in the 1990s to glorify networking technology
. This narrative, he argues, erased alternative visions of digital society and became a powerful contemporary
myth. By analyzing historical case studies (e.g. early web projects and civic networks), Bory “decodes and
challenges the foundations of [our] network ideologies”
. The book has a robust citation network in media studies and offers Gorgonwars a socio-historical perspective
on how digital infrastructures carry hidden ideological allegories (e.g. the internet as a liberating “hero” of
modern mythology).
Vivian Asimos (2022), Digital Mythology and the Internet’s Monster: The Slender Man – An innovative study
that uses the Slender Man horror meme as an extended case of digital-age mythmaking. Asimos employs
structural anthropology and online ethnography to show “how the Slender Man mythology is structured, and
how its everlasting nature in online communities demonstrates [the] importance of the mythos.”
 In other words, a crowd-sourced monster legend functions like a modern folk mythology, revealing the role of
digital communities in collectively crafting and sustaining narratives. This book (Bloomsbury Academic)
exemplifies comparative mythology in a contemporary key: it compares ancient mythic functions (providing
meaning, identity, cautionary tales) with the participatory storytelling on forums and wikis. For Gorgonwars,
Asimos’s work underscores how polymorphic myths can emerge in cyberspace, where authorship is communal
and symbols (like the faceless Slender Man) evolve through dialectical interaction between users and culture.
Jodi Dean (2005), Communicative Capitalism – (See above in Neoliberal Subjectivity.) Dean’s concept is also
crucial to digital mythology: it explains why social media’s structure – endless circulation of messages, “likes,”
and trends – tends to absorb and neutralize dissenting narratives. In the context of Gorgonwars, one can read
communicative capitalism as a cautionary meta-myth: the myth of participation (that simply sharing content
equals meaningful action) is itself an ideology that needs demystifying. Dean’s analysis of networked
communication provides a theoretical backbone for critiquing the “hivemind” tendencies of online discourse
and for understanding how digital platforms can both create new myths and constrain imaginative alternatives.
Each of these works offers deep, reflective insights and is supported by substantial citation networks or rich
analysis. Together they form a comprehensive foundation for the Gorgonwars project, addressing its core
themes while deliberately moving beyond the usual canon. By engaging with these authors, one can glean
fresh perspectives on how myths and narratives — from ancient allegories to Instagram memes — shape
human subjectivity and social realities under neoliberal and digital conditions. The selected literature not only
critiques prevailing ideologies but also exemplifies the original, critical thinking that Gorgonwars aims to
embody. Sources:

Excellent. Based on your clarification, here is a focused, methodological list of questions meant to refine your
Polymorphous Mythology PhD framework by interrogating the key philosophical tension between Jung and
Hegel (mediated through Jameson), with Whitehead as a potential bridge. These questions aim to help you
resolve foundational methodological contradictions and locate where your system draws its conceptual
coherence.

I. Jung vs. Hegel (via Jameson): Methodological Conflict
A. Subjectivity and Dialectics
Does Jung’s model of individuation, rooted in symbolic integration and unconscious archetypes, allow for
historical development, or is it fundamentally ahistorical compared to Hegel’s dialectic of Spirit?

If Hegel’s subject is formed through contradiction and negation in history, can this model accommodate
archetypes, or does it flatten them into rational categories?

Jameson reads the unconscious as "the political unconscious": how does this align or conflict with Jung’s
notion of a collective unconscious that is not historical but archetypal?

Are Jungian archetypes compatible with totalizing mediation, or are they fragments that resist being
synthesized into a dialectic?

B. Myth vs. History
Is myth in Jung a timeless structural expression of psyche, whereas in Hegel myth is sublated into concept
(Begriff) through rational mediation?

Can myth, for Jung, ever be “overcome” in the way Hegel overcomes narrative by systematizing Spirit? Or is
myth eternally recurrent and irreducible?

Is Jameson’s insistence that “narrative is the symbolic resolution of real contradictions” fundamentally at odds
with Jung’s view that narrative is the symbolic resolution of internal psychic fragmentation?

C. Temporality and Process
How does Hegel’s concept of time as historical unfolding differ from Jung’s more cyclical, alchemical time? Are
these views reconcilable in a polymorphous framework?

Does Whitehead’s process philosophy (with his notion of concrescence and prehension) offer a middle path
between Hegelian teleology and Jungian symbol formation?

II. Whitehead as Bridge Figure
A. Process and Symbol
Does Whitehead’s notion of “eternal objects” parallel Jung’s archetypes, and if so, does he historicize them in a
way that makes them Hegelian-compatible?

Can Whitehead’s concept of actual occasions provide a more fluid methodological category than Hegel’s static
sublations or Jung’s rigid archetypes?
Is Whitehead’s notion of the “ingression” of eternal objects into temporal events a way to map Jung’s symbols
into a Hegelian historical-dialectical field?

B. Experience and Aesthetics
How does Whitehead’s valuation of aesthetic experience and symbolic resonance align with Jung’s emphasis
on the imaginal? Is this where Hegel’s conceptual violence breaks down?

Can Whitehead’s metaphysical openness to multiple layers of reality function as a cosmological ground for
polymorphous mythology itself?

III. Polymorphous Mythology as Methodology
A. Integration or Conflict?
Is polymorphous mythology a synthetic methodology, aiming to reconcile Jung and Hegel through Whitehead?
Or is it a dialectical battlefield where these systems remain in tension?

Can the methodology tolerate incommensurability, or must it seek formal synthesis?

Are the “myths” of polymorphous mythology best understood as symbolic (Jungian) or as ideological/narrative
totalities (Jamesonian/Hegelian)?

B. Narrative Formation
What counts as a myth in this methodology—an archetypal symbol (Jung), a dialectical contradiction (Hegel),
or an ideologeme (Jameson)?

Does polymorphous mythology generate myths, or does it excavate and recontextualize them?

What is the status of metaphor versus allegory in this methodology? Is allegory always historical-materialist
(Jameson), or can it also be alchemical/symbolic?

IV. Critical Stakes and Antidotes to Gorgonification
A. Degeneration of Symbolic Systems
Does Hegelian dialectic risk Gorgonification through over-systematization (turning everything to stone through
total mediation)?

Does Jung risk it through symbolic stagnation, by repeating fixed archetypes with no historical inflection?

Is Whitehead’s process cosmology the only one of the three that maintains flow and multiplicity, preventing
petrification?

B. Critique of Language and Expression
How does each philosopher deal with the limits of language? Does Jung’s mythopoesis, Hegel’s logic, or
Whitehead’s metaphysical symbolism provide the richest grammar for resisting hivemindidioms?

Is it possible to develop a symbolic literacy through this triangulated methodology that resists neoliberal
flattening of meaning?

Would you like me to structure these into a formal dissertation outline or rubric next? Or refine one strand of
debate (e.g., Jungian symbol vs. Jamesonian allegory) into a core methodological defense?

Mephistodata Protocol Rules from Our Conversation:

alright so ive been thinking maybe to add a mezo logic - mezo logic would be like, ok so lets say star wars says
"the force" works like this and that, then another show, or religion, says something similar about a similar force,
lets say DBZ; so mezo logic would be the degree to which the internal, external, AND "between fictional/ (or

Yes — I follow exactly what you mean, and mezo logic as you’re describing it makes sense as a third analytical
layer between internal logic and external logic.

Here’s a breakdown and scrutiny:

1. Definition Precision
Internal logic = the coherence of a concept/system within its own world or ruleset.

External logic = the coherence of that concept/system when evaluated from outside that world.

Mezo logic = the coherence/correspondence of a concept/system between worlds — fictional to fictional,
fictional to nonfictional, or nonfictional to nonfictional — without collapsing them into one frame.

This isn’t just “cross-referencing” — it’s about evaluating the structural resonance of concepts across separate
ontological spaces.

2. What Mezo Logic Does Well
Finds archetypal continuity: e.g., comparing “The Force” in Star Wars with Dragon Ball Z’s ki, or with Buddhist
prana, and mapping points of convergence/divergence.

Exposes cultural patterning: reveals how similar logics propagate across different mediums, genres, and even
ideological systems.

Builds a meta-lexicon: allows you to catalog parallel concepts in different worlds (mythic, fictional, political,
religious) without reducing them to the same thing.

3. Potential Pitfalls
Over-homogenization: treating all resonances as equivalence rather than similarity with differences.

Source contamination: importing rules from one world that break the internal logic of another.

Infinite regress: mezo comparisons can proliferate endlessly without producing actionable insight unless
bounded by purpose (e.g., building a typology).

4. How It Fits Our Framework
Mezo logic would operate in Mephistodata protocol as a bridge-layer reasoning — letting AI or researcher map
how concepts replicate, mutate, and survive across domains.

It complements fuzzy logic boundaries because it resists binary “same/different” classification, instead allowing
for degrees of correspondence.

It supports egregore warfare mapping, since egregores often spread via mezo pathways — migrating from one
fictional/mythic frame into another cultural context.

Right — in that framing, mezo logic isn’t just “compare things between worlds” but is actually a test for
cross-world compatibility.

It becomes something like:

Mezo Logic
A reasoning layer that examines whether two or more systems from different worlds (fictional or nonfictional)
can coexist in a shared framework without breaking their respective internal logics.

If compatible: there’s genuine mezo overlap — like Dragon Ball Z’s qi and Star Wars’ The Force both being
life-energy systems that allow for mind-body enhancements without invalidating either’s core rules.

If incompatible: no mezo logic exists — like trying to merge Matrix reality-bending with Tolkien’s Middle-earth
magic, where the ontology of “simulation” vs. “divine creation” would collapse each other’s narrative structure.

Practical use:

For fiction-writing: tells you if crossover or mythic synthesis is plausible without logical fracture.

For cultural analysis: shows how mythic or ideological systems migrate and integrate across different mediums.

For Mephistodata protocol: acts as a middle filter between internal and external logic checks, preventing
“source contamination” while mapping potential synthesis pathways.

If we integrate this into the methodology stack, we’d have:

Internal Logic – Is it consistent in its own world?

Mezo Logic – Can it coexist with another world’s rules without collapse?

External Logic – Does it hold up against outside-world reasoning or evidence?

I can see this slotting into Mephistodata as a structural compatibility index.
Do you want me to actually formalize mezo logic into a scoring system so it’s testable instead of just
descriptive? That would make it a research tool, not just a concept.
